A relaxation viewpoint to Unbalanced Optimal Transport: duality, optimality and Monge formulation
Giuseppe Savaré, Giacomo Enrico Sodini

Abstract
. We present a general convex relaxation approach to study a wide class of Unbalanced Optimal Transport problems for finite non-negative measures with possibly different masses. These are obtained as the lower semicontinuous and convex envelope of a cost for non-negative Dirac masses. New general primal-dual formulations, optimality conditions, and metric-topological properties are carefully studied and discussed.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 49Q22, 28A33 ; Secondary: 49K27.

1.
Introduction

The problem of extending Optimal Transport methods to pairs of unbalanced non-negative measures has been considered in a large number of works with different techniques and different aims.

For what concerns dynamical formulations, many models inspired by the Benamou-Brenier’s
[
BB00
]

fluid dynamic formulation of the classical Kantorovich-Rubinstein-Wasserstein Optimal Transport metric have been proposed, see for example
[
KMV16
,
Maa+15
,
LM15
,
PR14
,
PR16
,
Chi+18
,
Chi+18a
,
LMS18
,
LM19
]
. In such works, the authors consider source terms in the continuity equation, thus leading to gain/loss of mass during the evolution. The models proposed differ in the kind of source or penalization. We refer to
[
Chi+18
]
where a more detailed description of these models is given.

Static formulations of the Unbalanced Optimal Transport problem were proposed already by Kantorovich and Rubinstein
[
KR58
]
and subsequently extended by Hanin
[
Han99
]
(see also the dual norm in
[
Han92
]
). These approaches can be thought as a classical Optimal Transport problem where a fraction of the mass is allowed to go (or come from) a point at infinity (see also
[
FG10
,
Gui02
]
). More recent approaches are given by the so called optimal partial transport
[
CM10
,
Fig10
]
, which was previously related to image retrieval
[
PW08
,
RGT97
]
.

Optimal partial transport (see
[
Chi+18
]
) is in turn also related to
[
PR14
,
PR16
]
, since this latter works also provide a dynamic formulation of optimal partial transport. We also mention that
[
PR14
,
PR16
]
are also connected to
[
Ben03
]
where it was proposed to change the marginal constraints and to add a penalization term.

The important case of the Hellinger-Kantorovich metric (a.k.a. Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao), somehow interpolating the Wasserstein and the Hellinger metrics,
has been proposed independently in
[
KMV16
,
Chi+18
,
Chi+18a
,
LMS18
]
, with different equivalent characterizations.

A very useful one
[
LMS18
]
involves an entropic relaxation of the marginal constraints in the classical static formulation of Optimal Transport,
it provides a paradigmatic example of the wide class of Optimal Entropy-Transport problems, and suggests the crucial role of the so-called cone geometry.

[
Chi+18a
]
introduces a general class of Unbalanced Optimal Transport problems in compact subsets of some Euclidean space induced by a sublinear cost function depending on positions and masses, using a suitable static semi-coupling formulation and proving a general duality result, with interesting applications to various dynamic problems.

It turns out that all these different viewpoints are specific examples of
a general construction based on convex relaxation, which we want to exploit in the present paper.

A convex relaxation viewpoint.

Adopting the same approach of
[
SS22
]
used for the classical Optimal Transport problem and extending some ideas already contained in
[
LMS18
]
, the aim of this work is to define
a general class of Unbalanced Optimal Transport problems
between non-negative measures
as the
convex and lower semicontinuous envelope
of a cost initially defined only between weighted Dirac masses, and then to study the corresponding duality formulas, optimality conditions and metric-topologial properties.

Let us briefly recall the result of
[
SS22
]
which is the starting point for the present work; given a pair of completely regular spaces
\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
\mathsf{X}_{2}
, every proper and lower semicontinuous cost function
\mathsf{c}:\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}\to[0,+\infty]
can be naturally lifted to a singular cost functional
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{c}}:\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\to[0,+\infty]
,
where
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
denote the
space of signed and finite Radon measures in

\mathsf{X}_{i}
.

\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{c}}
is finite only between balanced pairs of Dirac masses
and can be defined as

(1.1)

\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{c}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\begin{cases}r\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2})\quad&\text{ if }\begin{aligned} &\quad\mu_{1}=r\delta_{x_{1}},\,\mu_{2}=r\delta_{x_{2}},\\
&\quad x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1},x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2},\,r\geq 0,\end{aligned}\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ elsewhere.}\end{cases}

By
[
SS22
, Theorem 4.4]

the convex and lower semicontinuous (w.r.t. the product weak topology) envelope of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{c}}
coincides with the
Optimal Transport functional

(1.2)

\mathsf{OT}_{\mathsf{c}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\inf\Big\{\int\mathsf{c}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}:\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\Big\}&\text{ if }\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X_{i}),\ \mu_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1})=\mu_{2}(\mathsf{X}_{2}),\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ elsewhere},\end{cases}

where
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
denotes
the cone of nonnegative (finite Radon) measures in
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
and

\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is the subset
of couplings between
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, i.e. measures in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2})

with marginals
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
respectively.
Clearly
\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is empty
if
\mu(\mathsf{X}_{1})\neq\mu_{2}(\mathsf{X}_{2}).

In order to extend this construction to the case of non-negative measures with possibly different masses, we represent weighted Dirac masses in
\mathsf{X}

of the form

r\delta_{x}
,
r\geq 0
, as points
(x,r)
of the so called geometric cone
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]:=\big(\mathsf{X}\times[0,+\infty)\big)/\sim

(see Section
2.1
),
where the equivalence relation
\sim
identifies all the points of the form

(x,0)
,
x\in\mathsf{X}
, which
correspond to the null measure in
\mathsf{X}
.
A cost on weighted Dirac masses can thus
be expressed by a function

\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty],

which we will assume to be proper, lower semicontinuous and
radially
1
-homogeneous
, in the sense that the map

\mathbb{R}_{2}^{+}\ni(r_{1},r_{2})\mapsto\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

is
1
-homogeneous for every fixed
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}
. While the properness and the lower semicontinuity assumptions are natural, the
1
-homogeneity assumption deserves a comment: from a modeling point of view we are saying that moving
mr_{1}\delta_{x_{1}}
to
mr_{2}\delta_{x_{2}}
costs exactly
m
times moving
r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}}
to
r_{2}\delta_{x_{2}}
. In analogy with (
1.1
) we can define the unbalanced singular cost
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}:\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\to[0,+\infty]
as

\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\begin{cases}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad&\text{ if }\begin{aligned} &\quad\mu_{1}=r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}},\,\mu_{2}=r_{2}\delta_{x_{2}},\\
&\quad x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1},x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2},\,r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0,\end{aligned}\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ elsewhere},\end{cases}

and look for
the largest convex and lower semicontinuous
functional
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
on
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
dominated by
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}
.
It turns out that
such a functional
admits an explicit characterization which
resembles (
1.2
)
via the formula

(1.3)

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}\mid\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right\},

where
\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is the set of
homogeneous couplings
,
i.e. measures
\bm{\alpha}
on
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
with
1
-homogeneous marginals

\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
(see (
2.12
)):

\bm{\alpha}
belongs to

\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
if

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mu_{1}(A_{1})&=\int_{A_{1}\times[0,+\infty)\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]}r_{1}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}(x_{1},r_{1};x_{2},r_{2}),\\
\mu_{2}(A_{2})&=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times A_{2}\times[0,+\infty)}r_{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}(x_{1},r_{1};x_{2},r_{2}),\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\text{for every Borel subsets $A_{i}\subset\mathsf{X}_{i}$.}

This choice has been inspired
by
[
LMS18
]
,
where the authors prove that
the class of Optimal Entropy-Transport problems
can be formulated precisely as in (
1.3
) for a suitable choice of the function
\mathsf{H}
(see in particular
[
LMS18
, Definition 5.1]
).

Structural properties of Unbalanced Optimal Transport problems

The following result collects some of
the fundamental properties
of the unbalanced framework (see Theorems
3.4
,
3.5
and
3.15
),
in parallel to similar results of the
classical Optimal Transport theory.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}
be completely regular spaces and let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
be a proper, radially
1
-homogeneous and lower semicontinuous function.

(1)

For every
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
such that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})<+\infty
, there exists an optimal homogeneous coupling

\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
such that

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}.

(2)

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is a lower semicontinuous convex function and satisfies

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}},r_{2}\delta_{x_{2}})=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

for every
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}
and every
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
, where
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)

is the l.s.c. convex envelope of
\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\cdot],[x_{2},\cdot])
with respect to the
variables
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}.

If, in addition,
\mathsf{H}
is also radially convex
(i.e. the map
(r_{1},r_{2})\mapsto\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])
is convex for every fixed
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}
), then the above inequality is an equality.

(3)

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is the convex l.s.c. envelope of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}
and admits the dual formulation

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\mathscr{U}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

\displaystyle=\sup\Big\{\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}\mid(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}\Big\}

for every
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
,
where

(1.5)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}:=\Big\{(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{2}):{}

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

\displaystyle\text{ for every }(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2},\,r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0\Big\}.

Notice that, if the cost function
\mathsf{H}
is given by

\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]):=\begin{cases}r\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2})\quad&\text{ if }r_{1}=r_{2}=r\geq 0,\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ elsewhere,}\end{cases}

for some proper and lower semicontinuous function
\mathsf{c}:\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}\to[0,+\infty]
, then
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}=\mathsf{OT}_{\mathsf{c}}
,
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{c}}
, and we recover precisely the analogous results for the classical Optimal Transport theory (see in particular
[
SS22
, Theorems 3.5 and 4.4]
).
The relaxation formula
(
1.4
)
extends the corresponding one
of
[
Chi+18a
, Proposition 3.11]
, showing in particular that the primal formulation
(
1.3
)
in terms of homogeneous couplings
is equivalent to the semi-coupling approach of

[
Chi+18a
]
.
(
1.4
) has also nice applications
to the structure of the Hellinger-Kantorovich metric,
see Remark
3.18
.

Along the same lines of the classical Optimal Transport theory, it is natural to investigate the

existence of optimal potentials maximizing
(
1.4
) and thus solving the dual problem.

We first focus on the class of continuous functions, when
\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}
are compact and metrizable and
\mathsf{H}
is finite and
continuous on the whole product cone
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
, radially
1
-homogeneous and convex.
In this case a sufficient condition relies on
the singular behaviour of the normal derivative of
\mathsf{H}

at the boundary of the product cone as in
the fundamental
example

(1.6)

\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]):=r_{1}+r_{2}-2\sqrt{r_{1}r_{2}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}/2},\quad x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,r_{i}\geq 0.

We refer to Section
4
for a detailed discussion of these hypotheses and Appendix
B
for an alternative set of assumptions (i.e. when
\mathsf{H}
is finite and continuous on an open conical domain).
In both these situations it is possible to define the analogue of the celebrated
\mathsf{c}
-transform for a pair
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
as

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2})

\displaystyle:=\inf_{x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}}\inf_{\alpha\geq 0}\biggl\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2},1])-\alpha\varphi_{1}(x_{1})\biggr\},\quad x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2},

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle:=\inf_{x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}}\inf_{\alpha\geq 0}\biggl\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},\alpha])-\alpha\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2})\biggr\},\quad x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1},

and prove that the transformed potentials are equicontinuous so that the use of a compactness argument
in the space
\mathrm{C}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathrm{C}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
is possible. This will produce an optimal pair of potentials, see Theorems
4.7
and
B.3
.

Optimality conditions and relaxed duality

In this unbalanced setting, general optimality conditions still involve the notion of cyclical monotonicity: a subset
\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone if for every finite family of points
\{(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset\Gamma
and every permutation
\sigma
of
\{1,\dots,N\}
it holds

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{\sigma(i)}).

Similarly to the classical Optimal Transport case,
every optimal homogeneous coupling is concentrated on a
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone set
\Gamma
(see Proposition
5.2
) which in addition is
a radial convex cone
in the sense that

([x_{1},r_{1}^{i}],[x_{2},r_{2}^{i}])\in\Gamma,\,\lambda_{i}\geq 0,\,i=1,2\,\Rightarrow\Big(\big[x_{1},\sum_{i=1}^{2}\lambda_{i}r_{1}^{i}],[x_{2},\sum_{i=1}^{2}\lambda_{i}r_{2}^{i}]\Big)\in\Gamma.

This further property comes from the radial homogeneity and convexity assumption on
\mathsf{H}
.

Formulating a converse statement to the above proposition, thus involving sufficient optimality conditions, requires additional assumptions on the compatibility between the radial cone
\Gamma
, on which an admissible homogeneous coupling
\bm{\alpha}
between measures
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
is concentrated, and the cost function
\mathsf{H}
: in Section
5.1
we study the natural directed graph structures induced by
\mathsf{H}
and
\Gamma
, similarly to what has been done in the classical Optimal Transport theory
[
Bei+09
,
BC10
]
for possibly infinite costs. This leads to the notion of
\mathsf{H}
-connectedness:
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-connected if, whenever
\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}_{1}\in\pi^{1}(\Gamma)
(the projection of
\Gamma
on the first cone
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]
), we can find a sequence of points
\{\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{1},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{2},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{2},\dots,\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{N},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{N}\}
such that

(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{1}),(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{i})\in\Gamma,\,i=2,\dots,N,\quad\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{N}),\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i+1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{i})<+\infty,\,i=1,\dots,N-1.

In other words, the sequence
\{\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{1},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{2},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{2},\dots,\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{N},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{N}\}
“connects”
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
to
\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}_{1}
keeping the cost finite when moving from a point in
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
to a point in
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]
and imposing that the considered pair is in
\Gamma
, when moving from a point in
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]
to a point in
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
. For example, if
\mathsf{H}
is everywhere finite, then any
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-connected (see Theorem
5.8
for simple conditions implying
\mathsf{H}
-connectedness).

Upon assuming that
\Gamma
is a
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone and
\mathsf{H}
-connected subset of the effective domain of
\mathsf{H}
also intersecting the interior of such domain, it is possible to prove the existence of
relaxed optimal potentials
: these are Borel functions
\varphi_{i}:\mathsf{X}_{i}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
satisfying

(1.7)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+_{o}\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{ for every }x_{i}\in\mathsf{X}_{i},\,r_{i}\geq 0,

(1.8)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{ if }([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\Gamma,

where the inequality in (
1.7
) corresponds to the constraint as in (
1.5
) and the notation
+_{o}
means that, whenever an ambiguity
\pm\infty\mp\infty
arises, the sum is set equal to
0
.
This existence result, together with some finiteness conditions relating
\mu_{i}
and
\mathsf{H}
(see (
5.16
)), imply that

\varphi_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathsf{X}_{i},\mu_{i})

solve the dual problem

(1.9)

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}),

and yields
optimality for the homogeneous plan
\bm{\alpha}
. We refer to Theorem
5.10
for the detailed sufficiency result.

A Monge-like formulation via transport-growth maps.

As in classical Optimal Transport problems, Unbalanced Optimal Transport admits a more restrictive
Monge-like formulation in terms of
transport-growth
pairs
[
LMS23
]
:
they are maps
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})
from
\mathsf{X}_{1}
to
\mathsf{X}_{2}\times[0,+\infty)

acting on measures
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{1})
via the formula

(1.10)

(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}=\mathsf{T}_{\sharp}(\mathsf{g}\mu_{1}),\quad\mu_{2}=(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \mu_{2}(B)=\int_{\mathsf{T}^{-1}(B)}\mathsf{g}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}\quad\text{for every Borel set }B\subset\mathsf{X}_{2}.

Whenever
\mathsf{g}\in L^{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\mu_{1})
, a transport-growth
pair induces a
homogeneous coupling
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
,

\mu_{2}=(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}
, via the formula

(1.11)

\bm{\alpha}=([\text{id}_{\mathsf{X}_{1}},1],[\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g}])_{\sharp}\mu_{1},\quad\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=\int\mathsf{H}([x,1],[\mathsf{T}(x),\mathsf{g}(x)])\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x),

and one can study the corresponding Monge formulation of (
1.3
):

(1.12)

\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\inf\Big\{\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x,1],[\mathsf{T}(x),\mathsf{g}(x)])\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x):\mu_{2}=(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}\Big\}.

Clearly,
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}\leq\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{H}}
; notice also that, whenever an optimal plan
\bm{\alpha}
between
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
charges the set
\{\mathfrak{y}_{1}=\mathfrak{o}_{1}\}
, then it is impossible that such
\bm{\alpha}
can be induced by a transport-growth map
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})
as in (
1.11
). This corresponds to the fact that some of the mass of
\mu_{2}
does not come from
\mu_{1}
but it is “created”. Under the same hypotheses of the classical balanced case
[
Pra07
]
(i.e. the cost function is continuous and
\mu_{1}
is atomless),

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\mathsf{X}_{i}
be Polish spaces, let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
be a proper, radially
1
-homogeneous and continuous function,
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
with
\mu_{1}
diffuse (i.e.
\mu_{1}(\{x\})=0
for every
x\in\mathsf{X}_{1}
) and such that
\mu_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1})>0
. Then

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

In general, the infimum in the definition of
\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is not attained; however, as a consequence of the existence of relaxed optimal potentials as in (
1.9
) and (
1.7
), when
\mathsf{X}_{1}=\mathsf{X}_{2}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mu_{1}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
the Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the differential of
\mathsf{H}
satisfies suitable assumptions,
we also obtain the existence of an optimal transport-growth map
attaining the minimum in (
1.12
). This is the case, for example, of the cost function in (
1.6
) associated with the
Gaussian Hellinger-Kantorovich metric.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathbb{R}^{d}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathbb{R}^{d}]\to\mathbb{R}
be given by

\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]):=r_{1}+r_{2}-2\sqrt{r_{1}r_{2}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}/2},\quad x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,r_{i}\geq 0.

If
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mu_{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d})>0
for
i=1,2
and
\mu_{1}\ll\mathcal{L}^{d}
, then there exists a transport-growth map
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,+\infty)
such that

\mu_{2}=(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}=\mathsf{T}_{\sharp}(\mathsf{g}\mu_{1}),\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{H}([x,1],[\mathsf{T}(x),\mathsf{g}(x)])\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

The above theorem holds also for large class of cost functions
\mathsf{H}
, we refer to Theorem
5.13
for the general result.

Metric and topological properties.

Finally we frame in our general setting some of the results of
[
LMS18
,
Chi+18a
,
De 20
,
DM22
]
concerning the metric properties
of Unbalanced Optimal Transport functionals.
In particular, we
prove that, in case
\mathsf{H}
is (the
p
-th power of) a distance on
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]
, then the resulting cost
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is itself (the
p
-th power of) a distance on an appropriate subset of
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
metrizing the weak convergence of measures (see Theorems
A.4
and
A.5
), precisely as it is for the standard Optimal Transport problem
[
AGS08
, Proposition 7.1.5]
.

Plan of the paper.

Section
2
is devoted to establish the general setting and a few technical tools that will be used in the sequel.

Section
3
contains the core of our results: the convexification approach, some structural properties
and the comparison with the semi-coupling approach of
[
Chi+18a
]
,
the duality, and the Monge formulation.

Section
4
treats the dual attainment in spaces of continuous potentials under additional regularity assumptions on
\mathsf{H}
and on the spaces
\mathsf{X}_{i}
.

In Section
5
we present the general optimality conditions, the relaxed duality result and, as a consequence of the latter, the existence of optimal transport-growth maps.

Appendix
A
contains a few remarks on the metric and topological properties of
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
in case
\mathsf{H}
is (the
p
-th power of) a distance. Finally Appendix
B
reproduces the results of Section
4
under a different set of additional assumptions on
\mathsf{H}
.

The authors gratefully acknowledge the support of the Institute for Advanced Study of the Technical University of Munich, funded by the German Excellence Initiative.
G.S. has also been supported by IMATI-CNR, Pavia and by the MIUR-PRIN
202244A7YL project
Gradient Flows and Non-Smooth Geometric Structures with Applications to Optimization and Machine Learning
.

2.
Preliminaries, measures and functions on the cone

In this section,
\mathsf{X}
is a completely regular space (i.e. it is Hausdorff and for every closed set
C
and point
x\in\mathsf{X}\setminus C
there exists a continuous function
f:\mathsf{X}\to[0,1]
s.t.
f(x)=0
and
f(C)=\{1\}
). We denote by
\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X})
the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathsf{X}
and by
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
) the vector space of real valued (resp. the cone of non-negative) Radon measures on
\mathsf{X}
i.e. the countably additive set functions
\mu:\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathbb{R}
(resp.
\mu:\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X})\to[0,+\infty)
) s.t.

\text{ for every }B\in\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X})\text{ and every }\varepsilon>0\text{ there exists }K\subset B\text{ compact s.t. }\left|\mu\right|(\mathsf{X}\setminus K)<\varepsilon,

where
|\mu|
denotes the total variation measure of
\mu
.
We say that
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
is concentrated on a set

\Gamma\subset\mathsf{X}
if for
every
\varepsilon>0

there exists a Borel set
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}\subset\Gamma
such that

\mu(\mathsf{X}\setminus\Gamma_{\varepsilon})<\varepsilon
. The Radon property ensures
that if
\mu
is concentrated on
\Gamma
then it is concentrated on
a
\sigma
-compact subset
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Gamma
.

We denote by
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
the set of probability measures on
\mathsf{X}

i.e. the elements
\mu
of
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
s.t.
\mu(\mathsf{X})=1
. When
\mathsf{X}
is a Polish space (i.e. its topology is induced by
a metric
\mathsf{d}
such that
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{d})
is complete and
separable),
then every finite Borel measure is Radon so that

\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
coincides with the space of real valued Borel measures.

The zero measure in
\mathsf{X}
is denoted by
\bm{0}_{\mathsf{X}}
. We define the sets of non-negative discrete measures and non-negative weighted Dirac masses respectively as

\displaystyle\discr(\mathsf{X})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{i}\delta_{x_{j}}:\{c_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset[0,+\infty)\,,\,\{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset\mathsf{X}\,,\,m\in\mathbb{N}\right\},

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X})

\displaystyle:=\left\{r\delta_{x}:r\in[0,+\infty)\,,\,x\in\mathsf{X}\right\}.

Notice that
\discr(\mathsf{X}),\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X})\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
. If
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}
are two completely regular spaces and
f:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
is a Borel function we denote by
f_{\sharp}:\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{Y})
the push forward operator defined, for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
, as

f_{\sharp}\mu(B)=\mu(f^{-1}(B))\quad\text{ for every }\,B\in\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{Y}).

We denote by
\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X})
the vector space of continuous and bounded real functions.
There is a natural duality pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
between
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
and
\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X})

(2.1)

\langle\mu,\varphi\rangle:=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\mu\quad\text{for every }\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}),\ \varphi\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}).

(
2.1
) defines a real nondegenerate bilinear form in
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})\times\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X})
, for if a Radon measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
satisfies

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\mu=0
for every
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X})
,
then
|\mu|(B)=0
for every
B\in\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X})

(e.g. by the approximation result

[
Bog06
, Lemma 7.2.8]
) so that
\mu

is the null measure.
Hence we can endow
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
with
the weak Hausdorff topology
\sigma(\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}),\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}))
:
the coarsest topology on
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
for which the maps

\mu\mapsto\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\mu
are continuous for every
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X})
.
Since in general
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
is not first-countable (but
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
is Polish, thus metrizable, if
\mathsf{X}
is
Polish),
we will mostly deal with general nets
(\mu_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}
, i.e. maps
\lambda\to\mu_{\lambda}
defined in a
directed set
\mathbb{L}
with values in
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
, see
e.g.
[
Fol99
, §4.3]
.
By definition, a net
\left(\mu_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
converges to
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X})
in the weak topology if

\lim_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\lambda}=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\mu\quad\text{ for every }\,\varphi\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}).

If
f:\mathsf{X}\to(-\infty,+\infty]
is a function, we denote by
D(f)
its effective domain, defined as

D(f):=\left\{x\in\mathsf{X}\mid f(x)<+\infty\right\}

and by
\bar{f}
its lower semicontinuous envelope i.e.

(2.2)

\bar{f}(x):=\inf\left\{\liminf_{\lambda}f(x_{\lambda}):\left(x_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\subset\mathsf{X}\,,\,\,x_{\lambda}\to x\right\},\quad x\in\mathsf{X};

\bar{f}
is the largest lower semicontinuous function below
f
. If
\mathsf{X}
is also a topological vector space and
A\subset\mathsf{X}
, we denote by
\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(A\right)
(resp. by
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(A\right)
) the convex (resp. closed and convex) envelope of
A
. If
g:\mathsf{X}\to(-\infty,+\infty]
is a function, we denote its convex envelope and its closed convex envelope by
\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(g\right)
and
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(g\right)
, defined by

(2.3)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(g\right)(x)

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}g(x_{i}):\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathsf{X},\,\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in S_{n},\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}x_{i}=x,\,n\geq 1\right\},\ x\in\mathsf{X}

(2.4)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(g\right)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(g\right)},

where

S_{n}:=\left\{\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}:\alpha_{i}\in[0,1],\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}=1\right\}.

\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(g\right)
is the largest convex function below
g
and
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(g\right)
is the largest lower semicontinuous and convex function below
g
. The following proposition is a simple density result whose proof easily follows adapting
[
Bog06
, Example 8.1.6]
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a completely regular space. Then for every
c\geq 0
we have

\overline{\{\mu\in\discr(\mathsf{X}):\mu(\mathsf{X})=c\}}=\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}):\mu(\mathsf{X})=c\},\quad\overline{\discr(\mathsf{X})}=\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}).

Remark 2.2
.

It holds

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X})\right)=\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}).

This is an immediate consequence of the fact that
\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X})\right)=\discr(\mathsf{X})
and Proposition
2.1
.

The following Lemma is a refinement of Proposition
2.1
showing that, given a Borel function
f
and a non-negative measure
\alpha
, we can construct an approximating sequence of discrete measures for which we have convergence also of the integral of
f
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a completely regular space and let
\alpha\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
. Let
f:\mathsf{X}\to[0,+\infty]
be a Borel
function. Then there exists a net
\left(\bm{\gamma}_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\subset\{\mu\in\discr(\mathsf{X}):\mu(\mathsf{X})=\alpha(\mathsf{X})\}
s.t.

\lim_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\bm{\gamma}_{\lambda}=\alpha,\quad\lim_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}_{\lambda}=\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha.

Proof.

By Lusin’s theorem, we can find an increasing sequence of compact sets such that

\mathsf{X}_{k}\subset\mathsf{X}_{k+1}\quad\text{ for every }\,k\geq 1,\quad\alpha(\mathsf{X}\setminus\mathsf{X}_{k})\leq\frac{1}{k},\quad f\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$|$}_{\mathsf{X}_{k}}\text{ is bounded and continuous.}

Consider now the family of measures
\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}):\mu(\mathsf{X})=\alpha(\mathsf{X})\}
defined as

\alpha_{k}:=\frac{\alpha(\mathsf{X})}{\alpha(\mathsf{X}_{k})}\alpha\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$|$}_{\mathsf{X}_{k}}\quad\text{ for every }\,k\geq 1.

We can easily observe that

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\alpha_{k}=\alpha

indeed, if
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X})
, we have

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{k}=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\frac{\alpha(\mathsf{X})}{\alpha(\mathsf{X}_{k})}\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{\mathsf{X}_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\alpha

by monotone convergence. The same argument shows that we also have

(2.5)

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{k}=\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha.

By Proposition
2.1
, for every
k\geq 1
, we can find a net
\{\bm{\gamma}^{k}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}_{k}}\subset\discr(\mathsf{X}_{k})\cap\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}):\mu(\mathsf{X})=\alpha(\mathsf{X})\}
, such that

\lim_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}_{k}}\bm{\gamma}^{k}_{\lambda}=\alpha_{k}.

Moreover, since
f\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$|$}_{\mathsf{X}_{k}}
is bounded and continuous, it holds

\lim_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}_{k}}\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}^{k}_{\lambda}=\lim_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}_{k}}\int_{\mathsf{X}_{k}}f\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}^{k}_{\lambda}=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{k}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{k}=\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{k}.

This allows us to find, for every
k\geq 1
, some
\bar{m}(k)\in\mathbb{L}_{k}
s.t.

\left|\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}^{k}_{\lambda}-\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{k}\right|\leq\frac{1}{k}\quad\text{ for every }\,\lambda\geq\bar{m}(k).

Hence we can consider, for every
k\geq 1
, the directed sets
\mathbb{E}_{k}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}_{k}:\lambda_{k}\geq\bar{m}(k)\}
and the corresponding new sequence of nets
\{\bm{\gamma}^{k}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{E}_{k}}
. Obviously it holds

(2.6)

\lim_{\lambda\in\mathbb{E}_{k}}\bm{\gamma}^{k}_{\lambda}=\alpha_{k},\quad\left|\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}^{k}_{\lambda}-\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{k}\right|\leq\frac{1}{k}\quad\text{ for every }\,\lambda\in\mathbb{E}_{k}.

Define now the directed set

\mathbb{N}\otimes\mathbb{E}_{k}:=\{(k,\lambda):\lambda\in\mathbb{E}_{k}\}\text{ with order }(k,\lambda)\leq(k^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\iff k<k^{\prime}\text{ or }(k=k^{\prime}\land\lambda\leq\lambda^{\prime}).

By the diagonal principle for nets, we can find a directed set
\mathbb{B}
and a monotone final function

h:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{N}\otimes\mathbb{E}_{k},\quad h(\beta)=(h_{1}(\beta),h_{2}(\beta))\text{ with }h_{2}(\beta)\in\mathbb{E}_{h_{1}(\beta)}\quad\text{ for every }\,\beta\in\mathbb{B}

such that the diagonal net
\{\bm{\gamma}_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in\mathbb{B}}:=\{\bm{\gamma}^{h_{1}(\beta)}_{h_{2}(\beta)}\}_{\beta\in\mathbb{B}}\subset\discr(\mathsf{X})\cap\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}):\mu(\mathsf{X})=\alpha(\mathsf{X})\}
converges to
\alpha
. We only need to prove that also the integral of
f
converges:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}_{\beta}-\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}_{\beta}-\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{h_{1}(\beta)}\right|+\left|\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{h_{1}(\beta)}-\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}^{h_{1}(\beta)}_{h_{2}(\beta)}-\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{h_{1}(\beta)}\right|+\left|\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{h_{1}(\beta)}-\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{h_{1}(\beta)}+\left|\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{h_{1}(\beta)}-\int_{\mathsf{X}}f\,\mathrm{d}\alpha\right|,

where we have used (
2.6
). Now it is enough to observe that
h_{1}:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{N}
is a final monotone function i.e. it is an increasing monotone sequence converging to
+\infty
. Passing to
\lim_{\beta\in\mathbb{B}}
and using (
2.5
), we conclude.
∎

2.1.
The cone construction

It will be natural to state some definitions and results in the context of the so called geometric cone: we introduce on
\mathsf{X}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
the equivalence relation

(2.7)

(x,r)\sim(y,s)\quad\overset{\text{def}}{\Leftrightarrow}\quad\left[x=y,r=s\neq 0\quad\vee\quad r=s=0\right]

and the corresponding geometric cone
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]:=(\mathsf{X}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})/\sim
, whose points are denoted by gothic letters as
\mathfrak{y}
. We denote by
\mathfrak{p}
the quotient map
\mathfrak{p}:\mathsf{X}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]
sending a point
(x,r)
to its equivalence class
[x,r]
. Notice that
\mathfrak{p}
is just the identity map except for those points with
r=0
, which are all sent to the same equivalence class, the so called vertex of the cone, that we denote with
\mathfrak{o}
. We will consider the natural product operation on the cone given by

\lambda[x,r]:=[x,\lambda r]\quad\text{ for every }\lambda,r\geq 0,\,x\in\mathsf{X}.

In certain cases it is useful to
add a further isolated point
o

(which plays the role of a source or a sink)
to
\mathsf{X}
and to consider the spaces
\mathsf{X}_{o}:=\mathsf{X}\sqcup\{o\}
and

\mathsf{Y}_{o}:=(\mathsf{X}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})\sqcup\{(o,0)\}\subset\mathsf{X}_{o}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We can identify
\mathsf{X}
(resp.
\mathsf{X}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
)
with a (closed) subset of
\mathsf{X}_{o}
(resp.
\mathsf{Y}_{o}
) and

\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
) with the (closed) subset
of
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{o})
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{Y}_{o})
) given by
all the measures not charging
\{o\}

(resp.
\{(o,0)\}
).
Notice that
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]=\mathsf{Y}_{o}/\sim

and we can trivially extend
\mathfrak{p}
to
\mathsf{Y}_{o}
.

On the cone we introduce the projections on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\mathsf{X}_{o}
simply
defined as
\mathsf{r}([x,r])=r
and
\mathsf{x}([x,r])=x
if
r>0
and

\mathsf{x}([x,r])=\mathsf{x}(\mathfrak{o})=o
if
r=0
.

We can define a right inverse of
\mathfrak{p}:\mathsf{Y}_{o}\to\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]
as

\mathfrak{q}([x,r])=(\mathsf{x}([x,r]),\mathsf{r}([x,r])).

Notice that also that
\mathfrak{q}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}\})=\mathsf{X}\times(0,+\infty).

On
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]
we consider the following topology, weaker then the quotient one: a local system of neighbourhoods of a point
[x,r]
is just the image trough
\mathfrak{p}
of the local system of neighbourhoods given by the product topology at
(x,r)\in\mathsf{X}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, if
r>0
. A local system of neighbourhoods at
\mathfrak{0}
is given by

(2.8)

\left\{\left\{[x,r]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]:0\leq r<\varepsilon\right\}\right\}_{\varepsilon>0}.

If the topology of
\mathsf{X}
is induced by a metric
\mathsf{d}
, then the topology of
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]
is induced by the metric
\mathsf{d}_{\mathfrak{C}}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]\to[0,+\infty)
defined as

(2.9)

\mathsf{d}_{\mathfrak{C}}([x,r],[y,s]):=\left(r^{2}+s^{2}-2rs\cos(\mathsf{d}(x,y)\wedge\pi)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad[x,r],[y,s]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}].

With the above topology,
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]
is completely regular and it is the right object to consider when one wants to represent elements in
\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X})
; in particular we have the following result.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a completely regular space. Then
\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X})
is homeomorphic to
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]
.

Proof.

The homeomorphism is given by the map
\varphi:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]\to\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X})
defined as

\varphi([x,r]):=\begin{cases}r\delta_{x}\quad&\text{ if }r>0,\\
\bm{0}_{\mathsf{X}}\quad&\text{ if }r=0.\end{cases}

∎

Remark 2.5
.

Let us point out an obvious but useful fact that we will extensively use in the following without having to recall it further:
if a measure
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]\big)

does not charge
\{{\mathfrak{o}}\}

then

\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}{\bm{\beta}}

does not charge
\{o\}

and therefore we will identify it with a measure
in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
.
In particular, this construction does not depend on the choice of the point
o

and the map
\mathsf{x}
takes value in
\mathsf{X}

{\bm{\beta}}
-a.e.

If
R>0
, we define

(2.10)

\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}]:=\left\{[x,r]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]:0\leq r\leq R\right\}

and we will often identify measures on
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]
with support contained in
\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}]
with elements of
\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}])
.
For every
p\geq 1
, we introduce moreover the set

\mathcal{\mathfrak{M}}_{+}^{p}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]):=\left\{\alpha\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]):\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]}\mathsf{r}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha<+\infty\right\},

and the map (see the above Remark

2.5
)

\mathfrak{h}^{p}:\mathcal{\mathfrak{M}}_{+}^{p}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}])\to\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}),\quad\mathfrak{h}(\alpha)=\mathsf{x}_{\sharp}(\mathsf{r}^{p}\alpha).

We stress the fact that the map
\mathfrak{h}^{p}
does not depend on the point
o

occuring in the definition of
\mathsf{x}
.

We introduce now the product cone: given
\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
\mathsf{X}_{2}
completely regular spaces, we define
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]:=\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
endowed with the product topology. Points in the product cone are denoted by bold gothic letters as
\bm{\mathfrak{y}}=(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})=([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])
. We denote with
\mathfrak{o}_{i}

(resp.
o_{i}
) the vertex of
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}]
(resp. isolated points added to
\mathsf{X}_{i}

and forming the disjoint union
\mathsf{X}_{i,o}:=\mathsf{X}_{i}\sqcup\{o_{i}\}
),

i=1,2
and we set
\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}:=({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}),

\bm{o}:=(o_{1},o_{2}),

\mathfrak{C}_{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]:=\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}\}.

On the product cone we can consider the projections on the two
components

\pi^{i}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}]
sending
([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])
to
[x_{i},r_{i}]
and the projections on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and

\mathsf{X}_{i}
simply defined as
\mathsf{r}_{i}:=\mathsf{r}\circ\pi^{i}
and
\mathsf{x}_{i}:=\mathsf{x}\circ\pi^{i}
(
\mathsf{x}_{i}
maps
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
into

\mathsf{X}_{i,o}
).
In analogy with (
2.9
), if the topologies of
\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
\mathsf{X}_{2}
are induced by distances
\mathsf{d}_{1}
and
\mathsf{d}_{2}
respectively, the topology of the product cone is induced by the distance

(2.11)

(\mathsf{d}_{1}\otimes_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathsf{d}_{2})((\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}),(\mathfrak{w}_{1},\mathfrak{w_{2}})):=\left(\mathsf{d}_{1,\mathfrak{C}}^{2}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{w}_{1})+\mathsf{d}_{2,\mathfrak{C}}^{2}(\mathfrak{y}_{2},\mathfrak{w}_{2})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}),(\mathfrak{w}_{1},\mathfrak{w}_{2})\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}].

As in (
2.10
), given
R>0
, we define

\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]:=\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]=\left\{\bm{\mathfrak{y}}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]:0\leq\mathsf{r}_{i}(\bm{\mathfrak{y}})\leq R,\,i=1,2\right\}

and we will identify measures on
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
with support contained in
\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
with elements of
\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])
.
For every
p\geq 1
, we introduce the set

\mathcal{\mathfrak{M}}_{+}^{p}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]):=\left\{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]):\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\mathsf{r}_{1}^{p}+\mathsf{r}_{2}^{p})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}<+\infty\right\},

and the maps (see the above Remark
2.5
)

\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{p}:\mathcal{\mathfrak{M}}_{+}^{p}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])\to\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i}),\quad\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{p}(\bm{\alpha})=(\mathsf{x}_{i})_{\sharp}(\mathsf{r}_{i}^{p}\bm{\alpha})\quad i=1,2.

Finally we define, for every
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
and every
p\geq 1
, the set

(2.12)

\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\left\{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathcal{\mathfrak{M}}_{+}^{p}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]):\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{p}(\bm{\alpha})=\mu_{i},\,i=1,2\right\}.

If
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
, we say that
\mu_{1}
and

\mu_{2}
are the
p
-homogeneous marginals of
\bm{\alpha}
and that
\bm{\alpha}
is a
p
-homogeneous plan/coupling between
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.
Notice that if
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

then also
the restriction of
\bm{\alpha}
to

\mathfrak{C}_{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
,
\bm{\alpha}_{{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}}:=\bm{\alpha}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{C}_{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
,
belongs to
\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
.

Remark 2.6

(Trivial cases)
.

If
\mu_{i}(\mathsf{X}_{i})=0
for
i=1,2
(
\mu_{i}
are the null
measures in
\mathsf{X}_{i}
), then

\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\{\lambda\delta_{{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}}:\lambda\geq 0\}.

If
\mu_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1})>\mu_{2}(\mathsf{X}_{2})=0

then

\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\left\{\alpha\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}}:\alpha\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]),\ \mathfrak{h}^{p}(\alpha)=\mu_{1}\right\}.

A similar characterization holds
when

0=\mu_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1})<\mu_{2}(\mathsf{X}_{2}).

We say that
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is a non-trivial pair
if
\mu_{i}(\mathsf{X}_{i})>0
for
i=1,2
.

Remark 2.7
.

The class
\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

satisfies a natural invariance property with respect
to inclusion of the ambient spaces
\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
First of all,
if
\mathsf{X}_{i}
are subsets of
completely regular spaces

\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{i}

(and the topology of

\mathsf{X}_{i}
coincides with the
relative topology induced by the inclusion in
\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{i}
)
there is a natural
identification between
(pair of) Radon measures

\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})

and (pair of) Radon measures

\tilde{\mu}_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{i})

concentrated on
\mathsf{X}_{i}

(i.e. such that
\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{i}\setminus\mathsf{X}_{i}
is
\mu_{i}
-negligible).
Since
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
can be considered as a subset of

\mathfrak{C}[\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{1},\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{2}]
(with identification of the corresponding vertexes
\mathfrak{o}_{i}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{i}
)
every homogeneous coupling

\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

can be considered as a measure
in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{1},\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{2}])

which belongs to

\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\tilde{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2})
.
Conversely,
if
\tilde{\mu}_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{i})

are concentrated
in
\mathsf{X}_{i}

then every plan
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\tilde{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2})

is concentrated in

\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]

and its restriction to
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]

belongs to
\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

In particular, recalling
the construction
of the spaces
\mathsf{X}_{i,o}

at the beginning of Section

2.1
, for every
pair of measures
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})

the set

\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

can also be considered
as a subset of

\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1,o},\mathsf{X}_{2,o}]\big)
.

The following renormalization result comes from
[
LMS18
]
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\mathsf{X}_{i}
for
i=1,2
be completely regular spaces and let
p\geq 1
. Given
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])
and
\vartheta:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to(0,+\infty)
Borel measurable in
L^{p}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}],\bm{\alpha})
we can define

\displaystyle\prd_{\vartheta}(\bm{\mathfrak{y}}):=\left(\vartheta(\bm{\mathfrak{y}})^{-1}\mathfrak{y}_{1},\vartheta(\bm{\mathfrak{y}})^{-1}\mathfrak{y}_{2}\right),\quad\bm{\mathfrak{y}}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}],\quad\quad\quad\dil_{\vartheta,p}(\bm{\alpha}):=(\prd_{\vartheta})_{\sharp}(\vartheta^{p}\bm{\alpha}).

Then we have

\mathfrak{h}_{i}(\dil_{\vartheta,p}(\bm{\alpha}))=\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{p}(\bm{\alpha}),\quad i=1,2.

In particular, if we define

\vartheta_{\bm{\alpha},p}(\bm{\mathfrak{y}}):=\frac{1}{r^{*}(\bm{\alpha})}\begin{cases}\mathsf{r}_{1}^{p}(\bm{\mathfrak{y}})+\mathsf{r}_{2}^{p}(\bm{\mathfrak{y}})\quad&\text{ if }\bm{\mathfrak{y}}\neq(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})\\
1\quad&\text{ if }\bm{\mathfrak{y}}=(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})\end{cases},

where
r^{*}(\bm{\alpha})
is a normalization constant s.t.
\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\vartheta_{\bm{\alpha},p}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=1
given by

r^{*}(\bm{\alpha}):=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\mathsf{r}_{1}^{p}+\mathsf{r}_{2}^{p})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}+\bm{\alpha}(\{(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})\}),

we have that
\dil_{\vartheta_{\bm{\alpha},p},p}(\bm{\alpha})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])
, it has the same
p
-homogeneous marginals of
\bm{\alpha}
and its support is contained in
\mathfrak{C}_{r^{*}(\bm{\alpha})}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
.

The following Lemma is a compactness result for homogeneous marginals (the corresponding statement for classical marginals is
[
SS22
, Theorem 3.1]
).

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
p\geq 1
,
R>0
and let
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{X}_{i}
,
i=1,2
be completely regular spaces. Then:

(1)

if
\left(\alpha_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}
is a net in
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}])
such that
\mu_{\lambda}:=\mathfrak{h}^{p}(\alpha_{\lambda})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{L}
, converges to some
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
, then there exists a subnet
(\alpha^{\prime}_{\beta})_{\beta\in\mathbb{B}}
of
\left(\alpha_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}
convergent to some
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}])
with
\mathfrak{h}^{p}(\alpha)=\mu
;

(2)

if
\left(\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}
is a net in
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])
such that
\mu_{i,\lambda}:=\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{p}(\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
,

i=1,2
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{L}
, converge to some
\mu_{i}
in
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
,
then there exists a subnet
(\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}_{\beta})_{\beta\in\mathbb{B}}
of
\left(\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}

convergent to some

\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
.

Proof.

Thanks to
[
SS22
, Theorem 3.1]
, it is enough to prove only the first claim. Define, for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{L}
,

\vartheta_{\lambda}:=\mathfrak{q}_{\sharp}(\mathsf{r}^{p}\alpha_{\lambda})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}\times[0,R]).

Notice that this definition does not depend on the point
\bar{x}

w.r.t.
\mathfrak{q}
is defined. Observe that
\pi^{[0,R]}_{\sharp}\vartheta_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,R])
with mass bounded by
R^{p}
and

\pi^{\mathsf{X}}_{\sharp}\vartheta_{\lambda}=\mu_{\lambda}
. Then we can
apply
[
SS22
, Theorem 3.1]
to
\left(\vartheta_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}
and obtain that,
up to passing to a subnet, there exists

\vartheta\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}\times[0,R])
s.t.
\lim_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\vartheta_{\lambda}=\vartheta
. Now we define

O_{n}:=\left\{[x,r]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]:0\leq r\leq\frac{1}{n}\right\},\quad n\geq 1

and, for every
n\geq 1
, the nets of real numbers

m_{\lambda,n}:=\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}(O_{n}).

Observe that
0\leq m_{\lambda,n}\leq 1
for every
n\geq 1
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{L}
then, up to passing to a subnet (the same for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
), they converge in
\lambda\in\mathbb{L}
to some
m_{n}\in[0,1]
. Define then
m:=\inf_{n\geq 1}m_{n}
. We claim then that

\lim_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}^{p}}\mathfrak{p}_{\sharp}\vartheta+m\delta_{\mathfrak{o}}=:\bm{\alpha}.

Take any
\Omega\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]
open; if
\mathfrak{o}\notin\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\alpha_{\lambda}(\Omega)

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\int_{\mathsf{X}\times[0,R]}({\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{\Omega}\circ\mathfrak{q})(x,r)\frac{1}{(\mathsf{r}^{p}\circ\mathfrak{q})(x,r)}\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{\lambda}(x,r)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathsf{X}\times[0,R]}({\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{\Omega}\circ\mathfrak{q})(x,r)\frac{1}{(\mathsf{r}^{p}\circ\mathfrak{q})(x,r)}\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta(x,r)

\displaystyle=\bm{\alpha}(\Omega).

If, on the other hand,
\mathfrak{o}\in\Omega
, we have that
O_{N}\subset\Omega
for some
N\geq 1
; calling
\Omega_{n}:=\Omega\setminus O_{n}
(which is an open set), we have, for every
n\geq N
, that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}(O_{n})+\liminf_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}(\Omega_{n})

\displaystyle\geq\bm{\alpha}(\Omega_{n})+m_{n}.

Now we pass to the limit as
n\to+\infty
and, using the monotone convergence theorem and the fact that
\Omega_{n}\uparrow\Omega\setminus\{\mathfrak{o}\}
, we obtain

\liminf_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}(\Omega)\geq\bm{\alpha}(\Omega\setminus\{\mathfrak{o}\})+m=\bm{\alpha}(\Omega),

and this concludes the proof thanks to Portmanteau theorem (see e.g.
[
Bog06
, Corollary 8.2.10]
).
∎

2.2.
Functions on the product cone

We describe a few
properties of functions defined on the product cone that will be
useful in the sequel. In this subsection
\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
\mathsf{X}_{2}
are
completely regular spaces.
Recall that the indicator function

\mathsf{I}_{G}:X\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}

associated with
a subset
G
of a set
X
is defined by

\mathsf{I}_{G}(x):=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }x\in G\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},\quad x\in X.

Definition 2.10
.

Consider a function
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
and a subset
\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
.
For every
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}
we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}

\displaystyle:{}\,\,\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\to[0,+\infty]:\qquad(r_{1},r_{2})\mapsto\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x_{1},x_{2}}

\displaystyle:={}\Big\{(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\Gamma\Big\}.

We say that

•

\mathsf{H}
is radially

p
-homogeneous,
p\in[1,+\infty)
, if
\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}
is positively
p
-homogeneous for every
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}
i.e.

\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}(\lambda r_{1},\lambda r_{2})=\lambda^{p}\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})\quad\text{ for every }\lambda>0,\,(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2};

•

\mathsf{H}
is radially
convex if
\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}
is convex for every
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}
.

We say that
\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
is a radial cone
(resp. radial convex set) if its indicator function
is radially

1
-homogeneous (resp. radially convex).
Notice that we do not assume
that
a radially
p
-homogenoeus function
vanishes at
({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})

(or, similarly, that a radial cone contains
({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})
);
however such a property follows immediately
under lower semicontinuity and properness conditions, see
Remark

2.11
below.

We define the (radially)

1
-homogeneous, convex, and closed convex envelopes
\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right),\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right),\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right):\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
of
\mathsf{H}
as
(recall (
2.2
,
2.3
,
2.4
))

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

\displaystyle:=\inf_{\lambda\geq 0}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\lambda r_{1}],[x_{2},\lambda r_{2}]),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\right)(r_{1},r_{2})\quad\text{ for every }(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2},\,(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2},

\displaystyle\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}.

Similarly, in the case of
\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
, we define
\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\Gamma\right),\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Gamma\right),

and
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\Gamma\right)
so that
\mathsf{I}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\Gamma\right)}:=\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\mathsf{I}_{\Gamma}\right)
,

\mathsf{I}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Gamma\right)}:=\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathsf{I}_{\Gamma}\right)
and

\mathsf{I}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\Gamma\right)}:=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{I}_{\Gamma}\right)
respectively.

Remark 2.11
.

We added the terms
radial and radially
just to avoid
ambiguities in the case when
\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}
are linear spaces and
the notions of
1
-homogeneity and convexity could also refer to the joint behaviour of

\mathsf{H}
w.r.t. all the variables
x_{i},r_{i}
.
In this paper, we will
always interpret convexity and
1
-homogeneity w.r.t. the radial
variables
r_{i}
.

Notice that
\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\Gamma\right)
(resp.
\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Gamma\right),\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\Gamma\right)
)
is the set whose sections
\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\Gamma\right)_{x_{1},x_{2}}
are
the cone (resp. convex, closed convex) envelopes of the corresponding
sections
\Gamma_{x_{1},x_{2}}
of
\Gamma
. In particular, racalling Carathéodory
Theorem in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\Gamma\right):={}

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\lambda>0}\Big\{([x_{1},\lambda r_{1}],[x_{2},\lambda r_{2}]):([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\Gamma\Big\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Gamma\right):={}

\displaystyle\bigcup\Big\{\Big(\big[x_{1},\sum_{i=0}^{2}\alpha_{i}r_{1}^{i}\big],\big[x_{2},\sum_{i=0}^{2}\alpha_{i}r_{2}^{i}\big]\Big):([x_{1},r_{1}^{i}],[x_{2},r_{2}^{i}])\in\Gamma,\ \alpha_{i}\geq 0,\ \sum_{i=0}^{2}\alpha^{i}=1\Big\}.

We also note that if
\mathsf{H}
is proper (i.e. not identically
+\infty
), lower semicontinuous, and radially

1
-homogeneous, then
\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{0}_{1},\mathfrak{0}_{2})=0
.

The following result is a simple consequence of the
1
-homogeneity property.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\mathsf{X}_{i}
,
i=1,2
be completely regular spaces, let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
be a radially
1
-homogeneous Borel function
and let
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
. Then

\inf\left\{\int\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}:\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right\}=\inf\left\{\int\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}:\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{R(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])\right\},

where

(2.13)

R(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\mu_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1})+\mu_{2}(\mathsf{X}_{2}).

Proof.

It is of course enough to prove the
\geq
inequality. If
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
we can assume that
\bm{\alpha}(\{(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})\})=0

(if not, we can replace
\bm{\alpha}
with

\bm{\alpha}_{{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}}=\bm{\alpha}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{C}_{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
which has the same homogeneous marginals and a lower
\mathsf{H}
-cost,
since
\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})\geq 0
).
By Lemma
2.8
, we have that

\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}:=\dil_{\vartheta_{\bm{\alpha},1},1}(\bm{\alpha})\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{R(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])

and

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.13

(
1
-homogeneity and
q
-homogeneity)
.

Given
q\in[1,+\infty)
, we define the map
\mathsf{T}_{q}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
as

(2.14)

\mathsf{T}_{q}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]):=([x_{1},r_{1}^{1/q}],[x_{2},r_{2}^{1/q}]).

It is easy to check that
(\mathsf{T}_{q})_{\sharp}
is a bijective transformation from
\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
to
\mathfrak{H}^{q}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
for any pair
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
. Moreover, if
\mathsf{H}_{q}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,\+\infty]
is radially
q
-homogeneous, then
\mathsf{H}:=\mathsf{H}_{q}\circ\mathsf{T}_{q}
is radially
1
-homogeneous and

(2.15)

\inf\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{q}:\bm{\alpha}_{q}\in\mathfrak{H}^{q}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right\}=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}:\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right\}.

As an immediate consequence of the above formula,
many of the results of this work can also be stated in the case of
a
q
-homogeneous cost function
\mathsf{H}_{q}
, provided that
the unbalanced optimal transport functional is defined by using the
q
-homogeneous marginals
\mathfrak{h}^{q}

and the corresponding set
\mathfrak{H}^{q}

as in (
2.15
).
For this reason, in order to keep a simpler notation we will limit our analysis to the
1
-homogeneous case.

2.3.
Examples of cost functions

In this subsection we present some examples of functions
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
, where
\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
\mathsf{X}_{2}
are completely regular spaces, satisfying (some of) the hypotheses we will assume throughout the paper. See also the examples of
[
Chi+18a
, Section5]
.

Notice that given two radially
1
-homogeneous, convex, lower semicontinuous and proper functions
\mathsf{H}_{1},\mathsf{H}_{2}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty)
, and two lower semicontinuous and proper functions
\mathsf{c}_{1},\mathsf{c}_{2}:\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}\to[0,+\infty)
also the function

\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])=\mathsf{c}_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})\mathsf{H}_{1}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])+\mathsf{c}_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\mathsf{H}_{2}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

is radially
1
-homogeneous, convex, lower semicontinuous and proper. In this way, many other examples can be obtained starting from the ones presented below. Notice moreover
that the radial behavior of a
1
-homogeneous convex function

\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to[0,+\infty]
can be characterized by the reduced function and its recession slope

(2.16)

\mathsf{h}(x_{1},x_{2};g)=\mathsf{h}_{x_{1},x_{2}}(g):=\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}(1,g),\quad h^{\infty}_{x_{1},x_{2}}:=\lim_{g\to+\infty}g^{-1}\mathsf{h}_{x_{1},x_{2}}(g),

since

(2.17)

\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})=\begin{cases}r_{1}\mathsf{h}_{x_{1},x_{2}}(r_{2}/r_{1})&\text{if }r_{1}>0,\\
r_{2}\mathsf{h}^{\infty}_{x_{1},x_{2}}&\text{if }r_{1}=0,\ r_{2}>0,\\
0&\text{if }r_{1}=r_{2}=0.\end{cases}

2.3.1.
Mass-space product costs

We consider cost functions of the form

\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]):=\mathsf{H}_{+}(r_{1},r_{2})+\mathsf{H}_{-}(r_{1},r_{2})\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2}),\quad([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}],

where
\mathsf{H}_{+},\mathsf{H}_{-}:\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
are convex,
1
-homogeneous and lower semicontinuous and
\mathsf{c}:\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}\to[0,+\infty]
is a lower semicontinuous function satisfying

\mathsf{H}_{+}(r_{1},r_{2})+\mathsf{H}_{-}(r_{1},r_{2})\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2})\geq 0\quad\text{ for every }(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2},\,r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0.

Possible choices of
\mathsf{H}_{+}
and
\mathsf{H}_{-}
(to be then coupled with a suitable cost
\mathsf{c}
) are given by e.g.

(1)

\displaystyle m_{p}(r_{1},r_{2}):=\left(r_{1}^{p}+r_{2}^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\quad p\in[1,+\infty)
,

(2)

\displaystyle m_{p}(r_{1},r_{2}):=-m_{p}(r_{1},r_{2}),\quad p\in(-\infty,0)\cup(0,1)
,

(3)

\displaystyle m_{\infty}(r_{1},r_{2})=r_{1}\vee r_{2},\quad m_{-\infty}(r_{1},r_{2})=-(r_{1}\wedge r_{2}),\quad m_{0}=-\sqrt{r_{1}r_{2}}
,

(4)

\displaystyle n_{\alpha}(r_{1},r_{2}):=\frac{|r_{1}-r_{2}|^{\alpha}}{(r_{1}+r_{2})^{\alpha-1}},\quad\alpha\geq 1
,

(5)

\displaystyle|r_{1}^{\alpha}-r_{2}^{\alpha}|^{1/\alpha},\quad-|r_{1}^{\alpha}+r_{2}^{\alpha}|^{1/\alpha},\quad 0<\alpha\leq 1
.

In the various examples we are adopting standard conventions when the expressions are not defined. In particular, for
p<0
we set
m_{p}(r_{1},r_{2})=0
if
r_{1}r_{2}=0
,
n_{\alpha}(0,0)=0
.

2.3.2.
Homogeneous marginal perspective functional

Following
[
LMS18
, Section 5]
we can build
\mathsf{H}
starting from two entropy functions
F_{i}:\mathsf{X}_{i}\to[0,+\infty]
,
i=1,2
and a proper and lower semicontinuous cost function
\mathsf{c}:\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}\to[0,+\infty]
. Assuming that each
F_{i}
,
i=1,2
is convex, lower semicontinuous and finite in at least one positive point, we can define, for every number
c\in[0,+\infty]
, the function
\mathsf{H}_{c}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\to[0,+\infty]
, as the lower semicontinuous envelope of

\tilde{\mathsf{H}}_{c}(r_{1},r_{2}):=\begin{cases}\inf_{\theta>0}\left\{r_{1}F_{1}(\theta/r_{1})+r_{2}F_{2}(\theta/r_{2})+\theta c\right\},\quad&\text{ if }c\in[0,+\infty)\\
F_{1}(0)r_{1}+F_{2}(0)r_{2}\quad&\text{ if }c=\infty,\end{cases}\quad r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}.

The function
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
is then defined as

\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]):=\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2})}(r_{1},r_{2}),\quad([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}].

Such function
\mathsf{H}
is radially
convex and
1
-homogeneous (see
[
LMS18
, Lemma 5.3]
). Possible choices (see e.g.
[
LMS16
,
De 20
]
) for
F_{i}
are given by:

(1)

power like entropies: for
p\in\mathbb{R}
we define

U_{p}(s):=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{p(p-1)}\left(s^{p}-p(s-1)-1)\right)\quad&\text{ if }p\neq 0,1,\\
s\log s-s+1,\quad&\text{ if }p=1,\\
s-1-\log s,\quad&\text{ if }p=0,\\
\end{cases}\quad\text{ for }s>0,

with
U_{p}(0)=1/p
if
p>0
and
U_{p}(0)=+\infty
if
p\leq 0
;

(2)

indicator functions: for numbers
0\leq a\leq 1\leq b\leq+\infty
we define

I_{[a,b]}(s):=\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{ if }s\in[a,b],\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ if }s\notin[a,b];\end{cases}

(3)

{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}^{\alpha}
divergences: for a parameter
\alpha\geq 1
we define

{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}^{\alpha}(s):=|s-1|^{\alpha},\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

Some of the corresponding expression for
\mathsf{H}
are for example

(1)

In case of power like entropies with
F_{1}=F_{2}=U_{p}
:

\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{p}\left[(r_{1}+r_{2})-\frac{r_{1}r_{2}}{(r_{1}^{p-1}+r_{2}^{p-1})^{\frac{1}{p-1}}}(2-(p-1)\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2}))_{+}^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\right],\quad&\text{ if }p\neq 0,1,\\
(\sqrt{r_{1}}-\sqrt{r_{2}})^{2}+2\sqrt{r_{1}r_{2}}(1-e^{-\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2})/2}),\quad&\text{ if }p=1,\\
r_{1}\log(r_{1})+r_{2}\log(r_{2})-(r_{1}+r_{2})\log\left(\frac{r_{1}+r_{2}}{2+\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2})}\right),\quad&\text{ if }p=0.\end{cases}

In particular, in case
p=1
, for
\mathsf{X}_{1}=\mathsf{X}_{2}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
we can chose as cost functions
\mathsf{c}

\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{GHK}}(x_{1},x_{2}):=|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2},\quad\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{HK}}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\begin{cases}-\log(\cos^{2}(|x_{1}-x_{2}|))\quad&\text{ if }|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\pi/2,\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

The resulting cost functions
\mathsf{H}
are thus given respectively by

(2.18)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{GHK}}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

\displaystyle=r_{1}+r_{2}-2\sqrt{r_{1}r_{2}}e^{-|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}/2},

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{HK}}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

\displaystyle=r_{1}+r_{2}-2\sqrt{r_{1}r_{2}}\cos(|x_{1}-x_{2}|\wedge\pi/2).

(
2.18
) and
(
2.19
) are metrics on
\mathfrak{C}[\mathbb{R}^{d}]
, inducing the same canonical cone topology.
In particular,
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{HK}}
is related to (
2.9
) via the transformation in Remark
2.13

with
q=2
(apart from the specific value of the truncation constant).
Both functions are considered in
[
LMS18
]
and they generate the Gaussian Hellinger-Kantorovich and the Hellinger-Kantorovich metrics on non-negative measures, respectively.

(2)

In case of indicator functions with
F_{1}=F_{2}=I_{[a,b]}
:

\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])=\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{ if }\frac{a}{b}\leq\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}\leq\frac{b}{a},\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ else},\end{cases}

where
\frac{b}{a}=+\infty
if
a=0
and
\frac{a}{b}=0
if
b=+\infty
.

(3)

In case of the
\chi^{1}
divergence with
F_{1}=F_{2}=\chi^{1}
:

\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])=|r_{2}-r_{1}|+(\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge 2)(r_{1}\wedge r_{2}).

3.
Convexification and duality

This section presents the main convexification and duality results. In this section
\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
\mathsf{X}_{2}
are completely regular spaces.

3.1.
Unbalanced Optimal Transport as convex relaxation of
singular cost on Dirac masses

Definition 3.1

(Homogeneous conical formulation of Unbalanced Optimal Transport problems)
.

Let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
be a proper (i.e. not identically equal to
+\infty
) Borel function. We define the singular cost and the Unbalanced Optimal Transport cost
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}},\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}:\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\to[0,+\infty]
respectively as:

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad&\text{ if }\mu_{1}=r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}},\,\mu_{2}=r_{2}\delta_{x_{2}},\,(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2},\,(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2},\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ elsewhere}.\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\inf\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}:\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right\}\quad&\text{ if }(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2}),\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ elsewhere}.\end{cases}

The aim of this section is to study the relation between
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}
and
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
; in particular we are interested in studying the lower semicontinuous and convex envelope of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}
.

Let us first recall two simple properties in the following remarks.

Remark 3.2
.

The value of
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

does not depend on the ambient
spaces
\mathsf{X}_{i}
(recall the discussion of Remark

2.7
):
if
\mathsf{X}_{i}\subset\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{i}
,

\mathsf{H}
is the restriction
to
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]

of a function
\tilde{\mathsf{H}}

defined in
\mathfrak{C}[\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{1},\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{2}]
,
and
\tilde{\mu}_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\tilde{\mathsf{X}}_{i})

are the canonical extensions of
\mu_{i}
, then
Remark
2.7

yields

(3.1)

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\mathscr{U}_{\tilde{\mathsf{H}}}(\tilde{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2}).

In particular, we can always
“embed” Unbalanced Optimal Transport problems
in
\mathsf{X}_{i,o}
or, equivalently, suppose
that there is
at least one
\mu_{i}
-negligible
point
o_{i}
in each space
\mathsf{X}_{i}
.

Remark 3.3
.

If
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
is a proper Borel function, then

\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}}=\mathscr{S}_{\overline{\mathsf{H}}}.

Indeed, both are equal to
+\infty
outside the closed set
\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
and the equality on
\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
follows by Lemma
2.4
.

For this reason and to exploit Lemma
2.12
, we will usually assume that

(3.2)

\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]\text{ is a proper, radially $1$-homogeneous and l.s.c.\penalty\ function.}

In the following result we prove that the Unbalanced Optimal Transport cost
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is a lower
semicontinuous convex functional in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})

and the infimum in the Definition of
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is attained, when the
problem is feasible.

Theorem 3.4

(Existence of solutions
to the Unbalanced Optimal Transport problem)
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be as in
(
3.2
) and let
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
be as in Definition

3.1
.

(1)

For every

(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
such
that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})<+\infty
, there exists an optimal

1
-homogeneous coupling

\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{R(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])

not charging
{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}=({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})

such that

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha},

where

R(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is as in (
2.13
).

(2)

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}

is a lower semicontinuous convex functional
in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})

and satisfies

(3.3)

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}},r_{2}\delta_{x_{2}})\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

for every
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}
and every

(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
.

(3)

If, in addition,
\mathsf{H}
is also radially convex,
then (
3.3
) is an equality:

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}},r_{2}\delta_{x_{2}})=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]).

Proof.

(1)
Let
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
; by Lemma
2.12
, it holds

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}:\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{R(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])\right\}.

Thanks to Lemma
2.9
, we have that
\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{R(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])
is compact and the lower semicontinuity of
\mathsf{H}
gives that the functional

(3.4)

\bm{\alpha}\mapsto\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}

is lower semicontinuous. We can thus conclude that a minimizer exists
by the direct method in Calculus of Variations.
Possibly replacing
\bm{\alpha}
by

\bm{\alpha}_{{\mathfrak{o}}}=\bm{\alpha}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{C}_{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
we obtain an optimal homogeneous coupling not charging
({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}).

(2)
The convexity of
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
follows by the convexity of the
constraints and the linearity of the objective function characterizing

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
:
if
(\mu_{1}^{1},\mu_{2}^{1}),(\mu_{2}^{1},\mu_{2}^{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
and
t\in[0,1]
, we can take, thanks to point (1),
\bm{\alpha}_{1}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1}^{1},\mu_{2}^{1})
and
\bm{\alpha}_{2}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1}^{2},\mu_{2}^{2})
such that

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1}^{1},\mu_{2}^{1})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{1},\quad\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1}^{2},\mu_{2}^{2})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{2}.

It is then enough to observe that
\bm{\alpha}:=(1-t)\bm{\alpha}_{1}+t\bm{\alpha}_{2}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}((1-t)\mu_{1}^{1}+t\mu_{1}^{2},(1-t)\mu_{2}^{1}+t\mu_{2}^{2})
.

The lower semicontinuity of
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is a consequence of Lemma
2.12
: if
\{(\mu_{1}^{\lambda},\mu_{2}^{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
is a net converging to
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
, we can set
R:=\sup_{\lambda}R(\mu_{1}^{\lambda},\mu_{2}^{\lambda})
(see (
2.13
)) and consider, for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{L}
, some
\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1}^{\lambda},\mu_{2}^{\lambda})\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])
such that

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1}^{\lambda},\mu_{2}^{\lambda})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}.

We can thus use Lemma
2.9
to extract a
convergent subnet of
\left(\bm{\alpha}_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}
with limit
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
. Using again the lower semicontinuity of the
functional in (
3.4
), we can conclude that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is
lower semicontinuous.

(
3.3
) follows by the fact that

\bm{\alpha}=\delta_{([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])}

is an element of
\mathfrak{H}^{1}(r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}},r_{2}\delta_{x_{2}})
.

(3)
Let us assume that
\mathsf{H}
is radially convex and let

\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}},r_{2}\delta_{x_{2}})
be such that

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}},r_{2}\delta_{x_{2}})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha};

we observe that
\bm{\alpha}
is concentrated on

\big\{\lambda_{1}[x_{1},1],\lambda_{2}[x_{2},1]:\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\geq 0\big\}

with

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{r}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=r_{1},\quad\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{r}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=r_{2}.
