Hence, using Jensen’s inequality and the convexity of
\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(r_{1}\delta_{x_{1}},r_{2}\delta_{x_{2}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(\mathsf{r}_{1},\mathsf{r}_{2})_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha}

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(\mathsf{r}_{1},\mathsf{r}_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]).\qed

Thanks to Theorem
3.4
, given
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
such that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})<+\infty
, the set

(3.5)

\mathfrak{H}^{1}_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\left\{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right\}

is not empty.

In the following we give a direct proof that the Unbalanced Optimal Transport cost
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is the lower
semicontinuous convex envelope of the singular cost
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}
. This result will also
be obtained in Theorem
3.15
as a simple consequence of the dual characterization of
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
. However, the following proof highlights the role played by the discrete
1
-homogeneous marginals, which is not evident in the proof of Theorem
3.15
.

Theorem 3.5

(Convex l.s.c. envelope)
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be as in (
3.2
) and let
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}
and
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
be as in Definition
3.1
. Then

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}\text{ in }\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{2}).

Proof.

We only need to prove equality on
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
, being both functions equal to
+\infty

outside it. First of all let us compute
\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)
on

\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\right)=\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\right)\times\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\right)
(outside this set
\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)
is equal to

+\infty
).
We take
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\right)\times\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\right)

and we observe that any finite set in

\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
is always contained in
the cartesian product of two finite sets

M_{1}=\{\mu_{1}^{i}=r_{1}^{i}\delta_{x_{1}^{i}}:i=1,\cdots,I\}

and

M_{2}=\{\mu_{2}^{j}=r_{1}^{j}\delta_{x_{2}^{j}}:j=1,\cdots,J\}
,
so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})={}&\inf\Biggl\{\sum_{ij}\gamma_{ij}\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}(r^{i}_{1}\delta_{x_{1}^{i}},r_{2}^{j}\delta_{x_{2}^{j}}):\Big(r_{1}^{i},x_{1}^{i},r_{2}^{j},x_{2}^{j},\gamma_{ij}\Big)_{ij}\in A(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\Biggr\},\\
A(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):={}&\Biggr\{\Big(r_{1}^{i},x_{1}^{i},r_{2}^{j},x_{2}^{j},\gamma_{ij}\Big)_{ij}:(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\sum_{ij}\gamma_{ij}(r^{i}_{1}\delta_{x_{1}^{i}},r_{2}^{j}\delta_{x_{2}^{j}})\,,\,\sum_{ij}\gamma_{ij}=1,\gamma_{ij}\geq 0\Biggr\}.\end{split}

In particular it holds
that

\mu_{1}=\sum_{ij}\gamma_{ij}r_{1}^{i}\delta_{{x}_{1}^{i}},\quad\mu_{2}=\sum_{ij}\gamma_{ij}r_{2}^{j}\delta_{x_{2}^{j}}

for some
r_{1}^{i},r_{2}^{j}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and some
{x}_{1}^{i}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}
,

{x}_{2}^{j}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}
not necessarily distinct points.
Setting

(3.6)

\bm{\alpha}:=\sum_{i,j}\gamma_{ij}\delta_{([x_{1}^{i},r_{1}^{i}],[x_{2}^{j},r_{2}^{j}])}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])

one immediately checks that

(3.7)

\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}),\quad\sum_{ij}\gamma_{ij}\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}(r^{i}_{1}\delta_{x_{1}^{i}},r_{2}^{j}\delta_{x_{2}^{j}})=\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}.

Conversely, every
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])\cap\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

can be written as in (
3.6
)
with coefficients
(r_{1}^{i},x_{1}^{i},r_{2}^{j},x_{2}^{j},\gamma_{ij})_{ij}

in
A(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
; the integral identity of (
3.7
)
eventually shows that

\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)
can be written as

\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}:\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\cap\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])\right\}.

Reasoning as in the proof of Lemma
2.12
, we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}:\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\cap\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{f}}(\mathfrak{C}_{*})\right\},

where
\mathfrak{C}_{*}:=\mathfrak{C}_{R(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
, with
R(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
as in (
2.13
).
Thus

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\leq\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\quad\text{ for every }\,(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\right)\times\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\right).

Moreover
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is lower semicontinuous and convex hence, by definition of
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)
, it must hold

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\geq\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\quad\text{ for every }\,(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1}).

Then, in order to prove equality, we only need to prove the other inequality. To do so, fixed
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
, we prove that there exists a net
\{(\mu_{1}^{\eta},\mu_{2}^{\eta})\}_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\subset\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\right)\times\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\right)
s.t.
\lim_{\eta}(\mu_{1}^{\eta},\mu_{2}^{\eta})=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and a net

\{\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}\}_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\subset\discr(\mathfrak{C}_{*})\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{*})

s.t.
\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1}^{\eta},\mu_{2}^{\eta})
for every
\eta\in\mathbb{E}
satisfying

\lim_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{*}

where
\bm{\alpha}^{*}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{*})
(see (
3.5
)). To do so, we use Lemma
2.3
with
\mathsf{X}:=\mathfrak{C}_{*}
,
f:=\mathsf{H}
,
\alpha:=\bm{\alpha}^{*}
and we find
\{\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}\}_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\subset\discr(\mathfrak{C}_{*})\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{*})
s.t.

\lim_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}=\bm{\alpha}^{*},\quad\lim_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\int_{\mathfrak{C}_{*}}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}=\int_{\mathfrak{C}_{*}}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{*}.

Finally we can define

\mu_{1}^{\eta}:=\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{1}(\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}),\quad\mu_{2}^{\eta}:=\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{1}(\bm{\gamma}_{\eta})\quad\text{ for every }\,\eta\in\mathbb{E}.

Obviously
\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1}^{\eta},\mu_{2}^{\eta})
and
\mu_{i}=\lim_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\mu_{i}^{\eta}
, indeed if
\varphi_{i}\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\int_{\mathsf{X}_{i}}\varphi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}^{\eta}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\int_{\mathsf{X}_{i}}\varphi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{i}(\bm{\gamma}_{\eta})=\lim_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\varphi\circ\mathsf{x}_{i})\mathsf{r}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\eta\in\mathbb{E}}\int_{\mathfrak{C}_{*}}(\varphi_{i}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i})\mathsf{r}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}=\int_{\mathfrak{C}_{*}}(\varphi_{i}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i})\mathsf{r}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{*}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\varphi_{i}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i})\mathsf{r}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{*}=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{i}}\varphi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{i}(\bm{\alpha}^{*})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{i}}\varphi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i},

where we have used that
(\varphi\circ\mathsf{x}_{i})\mathsf{r}_{i}\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathfrak{C}_{*})
and the convergence of
\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}
to
\bm{\alpha}^{*}
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{*})
. Notice that, in general, it is not true that
(\varphi\circ\mathsf{x}_{i})\mathsf{r}_{i}\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])
.
Finally

\displaystyle\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

\displaystyle=\inf\biggl\{\liminf_{\lambda}\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1}^{\lambda},\mu_{2}^{\lambda}):\{(\mu_{1}^{\lambda},\mu_{2}^{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{L}}\subset\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\right),

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\lim_{\lambda}(\mu_{1}^{\lambda},\mu_{2}^{\lambda})\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\eta}\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1}^{\eta},\mu_{2}^{\eta})\leq\liminf_{\eta}\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{*}=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).\qed

Theorem
3.5

immediately yields the following
useful property.

Theorem 3.6

(Sublinearity of
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
)
.

The functional
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is sublinear
(i.e. convex and positively
1
-homogeneous) in

\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
:
for every
\mu_{i}^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})

and every
\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime\prime}\in[0,+\infty)

(3.8)

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\lambda^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime}+\lambda^{\prime\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime\prime},\lambda^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime}+\lambda^{\prime\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime\prime})\leq\lambda^{\prime}\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1}^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime})+\lambda^{\prime\prime}\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1}^{\prime\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime\prime}).

3.2.
Disintegration, barycentric projection and decomposition of homogeneous couplings

Every plan
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])

can be expressed as a superposition of
a Borel family of probability measures

(\alpha_{\bm{x}})_{\bm{x}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
,

\bm{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1,o}\times\mathsf{X}_{2,o}.

It is sufficient to consider
the map
\bm{\mathsf{x}}:=(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2}):\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to\mathsf{X}_{1,o}\times\mathsf{X}_{2,o}
,
where
\mathsf{x}_{i}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to\mathsf{X}_{i,o}
are defined in Section
2.1
,
and the plan

\bm{\gamma}:=(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2})_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1,o}\times\mathsf{X}_{2,o})
.
The disintegration of
\bm{\alpha}
w.r.t.
\gamma
yields the Borel family

(\alpha_{\bm{x}})_{\bm{x}}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
satisfying

(3.9)

\bm{\alpha}=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1,o}\times\mathsf{X}_{2,o}}\alpha_{x_{1},x_{2}}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}(x_{1},x_{2}),\quad\bm{\gamma}=\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha}.

Notice that if

\bm{\alpha}(\{{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}\})=0
then

\bm{\gamma}
is concentrated on
\mathsf{X}_{1,o}\times\mathsf{X}_{2,o}\setminus\{\bm{o}\}.

If moreover
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{1}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])
then
we can define

(3.10)

\bm{\varrho}:=(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}),\quad\varrho_{i}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}r_{i}\,\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{x_{1},x_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2}),\quad\varrho_{i}\in L^{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1,o}\times\mathsf{X}_{2,o},\bm{\gamma}),\,\,\varrho_{i}\geq 0.

Definition 3.7

(Barycentric projection and reduced couplings)
.

The barycentric projection
of a nontrivial plan
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{1}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])

is the plan
\bm{\alpha}_{b}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{1}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])

defined by

(3.11)

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}_{b}:={}\mathsf{T}[\bm{\varrho}]_{\sharp}\bm{\gamma},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathsf{T}[\bm{\varrho}](\bm{x}):={}\big([x_{1},\varrho_{1}(\bm{x})],[x_{2},\varrho_{2}(\bm{x})]\big),\quad\bm{x}\in\mathsf{X}_{1,o}\times\mathsf{X}_{2,o}.

When
\bm{\alpha}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])=0
(i.e.
\bm{\alpha}
is the null measure) we set
\bm{\alpha}_{b}:=\bm{\alpha}
.

We say that
\bm{\alpha}
is a reduced plan
if
\bm{\alpha}=\bm{\alpha}_{b}
or, equivalently, if
\alpha_{\bm{x}}
is a Dirac mass

\delta_{\bm{\varrho}(\bm{x})}

for
\bm{\gamma}
-a.e.
\bm{x}\in\mathsf{X}_{1,o}\times\mathsf{X}_{2,o}.

The interest in the barycentric projection
and in reduced couplings
is justified by the following result.

Proposition 3.8

(Reduced homogeneous couplings and minimizers)
.

If
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
,
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

then also
\bm{\alpha}_{b}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).
If
\mathsf{H}
satisfies (
3.2
)
and it is radially convex then

(3.12)

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{b}\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}.

In particular, if
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})<+\infty
, then the minimum of the Unbalanced Optimal Transport problem

3.1
is attained at a reduced
coupling.

Proof.

We can assume that
\bm{\alpha}
is nontrivial.
Let us first check that

\bm{\alpha}_{b}
preserves homogeneous marginals.
For every
\zeta_{i}\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
, we have

\displaystyle\int(\zeta_{i}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i})\mathsf{r}_{i}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{b}

\displaystyle=\int\zeta_{i}(x_{i})\varrho_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}(x_{1},x_{2})=\int\zeta_{i}(x_{i})\Big(\int r_{i}\,\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{x_{1},x_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})\Big)\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=\int(\zeta_{i}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i})\mathsf{r}_{i}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=\int\zeta_{i}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}.

By radial convexity, using Jensen’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1,o}\times\mathsf{X}_{2,o}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{x_{1},x_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\gamma(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1,o}\times\mathsf{X}_{2,o}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\varrho_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})],[x_{2},\varrho_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})])\,\mathrm{d}\gamma(x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{b}.\qed

We can now show that
it is possible to improve the previous representation by decomposing
the measures
\mu_{i}
in two parts, corresponding to complete or partial distruction/creation of mass.
We introduce the
partition
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}_{2}

of
\mathfrak{C}_{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
, where

\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}:=(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}\})\times(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}\}),\quad\mathfrak{C}_{1}^{\prime\prime}:=(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}\})\times\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}\},\quad\mathfrak{C}_{2}^{\prime\prime}:=\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}\}\times(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}\}).

Every homogeneous plan
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}^{1}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])

not charging
{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}=({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})

can be decomposed into the corresponding sum

\bm{\alpha}=\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}+\bm{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}_{1}+\bm{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}_{2}
,
where
\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}=\bm{\alpha}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}

and
\bm{\alpha}_{i}^{\prime\prime}:=\bm{\alpha}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{C}_{i}^{\prime\prime}.

Correspondingly we can write

\bm{\gamma}=\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}+\bm{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\bm{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}

(3.13)

\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}:=\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}),\quad\bm{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\{o_{2}\}),\quad\bm{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\{o_{1}\}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}).

Theorem 3.9

(Distinguished optimal couplings)
.

Let us suppose that
\mathsf{H}

satisfies (
3.2
)
and it is radially convex and let

\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
non trivial
such that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{1})<+\infty.

There exist Borel partitions
\{S_{i}^{\prime},S_{i}^{\prime\prime}\}

of
\mathsf{X}_{i}
and an optimal homogeneous and reduced
coupling

\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
not charging
{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}
such that
setting

(3.14)

\mu_{i}^{\prime}:=\mu_{i}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}S_{i}^{\prime},\quad\mu_{i}(S_{i}^{\prime})>0,\quad\mu_{i}^{\prime\prime}:=\mu_{i}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}S_{i}^{\prime\prime},

and keeping the above notation
for
\varrho_{i},\bm{\alpha}^{\prime},\bm{\alpha}_{i}^{\prime\prime},\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
we have

(3.15)

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}_{1}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{p}_{\sharp}(\mu_{1}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\delta_{1})\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}},

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}_{2}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\delta_{{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}}\times\mathfrak{p}_{\sharp}(\mu_{2}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\delta_{1}),

(3.16)

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}_{1}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\delta_{o_{2}},

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}_{2}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\delta_{o_{1}}\otimes\mu_{2}^{\prime\prime},

(3.17)

\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{i}(\bm{\alpha}^{\prime})=\mu_{i}^{\prime},\quad\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}=\bm{\gamma}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(S_{1}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime}),\quad

(3.18)

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}={}\mathsf{T}[\bm{\varrho}]_{\sharp}\bm{\gamma},\quad\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}={}\mathsf{T}[\bm{\varrho}]_{\sharp}\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\quad\mathsf{T}[\bm{\varrho}](x_{1},x_{2}):={}\big([x_{1},\varrho_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})],[x_{2},\varrho_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})]\big)

and

(3.19)

\varrho_{i}>0\text{ on $S_{1}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime}$},\quad\varrho_{1}=0\text{ on $S_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}$},\quad\varrho_{2}=0\text{ on $\mathsf{X}_{1}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime}$},

(3.20)

\varrho_{1}(x_{1},o_{2})=1\text{ on $S_{1}^{\prime\prime}$},\quad\varrho_{2}(o_{1},x_{2})=1\text{ on $S_{2}^{\prime\prime}$}\quad\text{$\bm{\gamma}$-a.e.}.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}=\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime}+\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
be an optimal reduced coupling
in
\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

not charging
{\bm{{\mathfrak{o}}}}
.

In the degenerate case when
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime}

vanishes, then
setting
\mu_{i}=m_{i}\nu_{i}
,

\nu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}_{i}),

m_{i}=\mu_{i}(\mathsf{X}_{i})>0,

we have

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},m_{1}],{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},[x_{2},m_{2}])\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{2}\geq\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},m_{1}],[x_{2},m_{2}])\,\,\mathrm{d}(\nu_{1}\otimes\nu_{2})

so that the reduced coupling

\bm{\alpha}=([x_{1},m_{1}],[x_{2},m_{2}])_{\sharp}(\nu_{1}\otimes\nu_{2})

satisfies all the assumptions
with
S_{i}^{\prime}=\mathsf{X}_{i}.

We can therefore suppose that
\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}

is nontrivial and we set

\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{i}(\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime}),\quad\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{i}(\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}_{i}^{\prime\prime}),\quad\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}:=\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{\sharp}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}},\quad\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}:=\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{\sharp}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime}:=\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}),

and we can write
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}=\mathsf{T}[\tilde{\bm{\varrho}}]_{\sharp}\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}

as in (
3.11
).

The optimal cost
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

can be decomposed as

(3.21)

\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\tilde{\varrho}_{1}],[x_{2},\tilde{\varrho}_{2}])\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{o}}_{2},[x_{2},1])\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}^{\prime\prime}.

Since

\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{1}(\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}_{2}^{\prime\prime})=\bm{0}_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}
and

\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{2}(\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}^{\prime\prime})=\bm{0}_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}

we have

\mu_{i}=\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime}+\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
:
we call
\theta_{i}^{\prime},\theta_{i}^{\prime\prime}

Borel representatives of the Lebesgue densities
of
\mu_{i}^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime\prime}
w.r.t.
\mu_{i}

and we call

S_{i}^{\prime\prime}:=\Big\{x_{i}\in\mathsf{X}_{i}:\theta_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})=1\Big\},\quad S_{i}^{\prime}:=\mathsf{X}_{i}\setminus S_{i}^{\prime\prime}.

Since
\theta_{i}^{\prime}+\theta_{i}^{\prime\prime}=1

\mu_{i}
-a.e. in
\mathsf{X}_{i}
, we clearly have

\theta_{i}^{\prime}=0

\mu_{i}
-a.e. in
S_{i}^{\prime\prime}

and

\theta_{i}^{\prime}>0

\mu_{i}
-a.e. in
S_{i}^{\prime}
.
We also have
\mu_{1}^{\prime}(S_{1}^{\prime\prime})=\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{1}(\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime})(S_{1}^{\prime\prime})=0

and since
\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{1}(\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime})=(\mathsf{x}_{1})_{\sharp}(\tilde{\varrho}_{1}\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime})

we deduce that
\tilde{\varrho}_{1}=0

\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}
-a.e. in
S_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}.

With a similar argument, we deduce that

\tilde{\varrho}_{2}=0

\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}
-a.e. in
\mathsf{X}_{1}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime}.

We define
\mu_{i}^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime\prime}
according
to (
3.14
).
Notice that
\mu_{i}^{\prime}=(\theta_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime}\ll\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime}
,
whereas
\mu_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}S_{i}^{\prime\prime}.

The quotients
q_{i}(x_{i}):=\theta_{i}^{\prime\prime}/\theta_{i}^{\prime}

are well defined
\mu_{i}
-a.e. in
S_{i}^{\prime}

and we have

\mu_{i}^{\prime}=(1+q_{i})\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime},\quad\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\mu_{i}^{\prime\prime}+\theta_{i}^{\prime\prime}\mu_{i}^{\prime}=\mu_{i}^{\prime\prime}+q_{i}\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime}.

We set

\varrho_{1}:=\begin{cases}\tilde{\varrho}_{1}(1+q_{1})&\text{in }S_{1}^{\prime}\times\mathsf{X}_{2},\\
0&\text{in }S_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathsf{X}_{2},\end{cases}\qquad\varrho_{2}:=\begin{cases}\tilde{\varrho}_{2}(1+q_{2})&\text{in }\mathsf{X}_{1}\times S_{2}^{\prime},\\
0&\text{in }\mathsf{X}_{1}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime},\end{cases}

and we define
\bm{\alpha}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
as in
(
3.15
). Finally

\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}:=\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(S_{1}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime}),\quad\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}:=\mathsf{T}[\bm{\varrho}]_{\sharp}\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}=\mathsf{T}[\bm{\varrho}]_{\sharp}\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\quad\bm{\alpha}:=\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}+\bm{\alpha}_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\bm{\alpha}_{2}^{\prime\prime}.

Let us check (
3.17
); since
\bm{\gamma}=\bm{\mathsf{x}}_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha}
, the equality
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}=\bm{\gamma}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(S_{1}\times S_{2}^{\prime})
is immediate, so that we have to prove that

\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{i}(\bm{\alpha}^{\prime})=\mu_{i}^{\prime}
.
We just consider the case
i=1
since the
calculations in the case
i=2
are completely analogous. For every test function
\zeta\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{1})
we get

\displaystyle\int(\zeta\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}}\zeta(x_{1})\varrho_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{1}^{\prime}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}}\zeta(x_{1})\tilde{\varrho}_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big(1+q_{1}(x_{1})\Big)\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{1}^{\prime}}\zeta(x_{1})(1+q_{1}(x_{1}))\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{1}(x_{1})=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\zeta\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}^{\prime}.

Let us now compute the
\mathsf{H}
-cost of
\bm{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\tilde{\varrho}_{1}+q_{1}\tilde{\varrho}_{1}],[x_{2},\tilde{\varrho}_{2}+q_{2}\tilde{\varrho}_{2}])\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\int\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\tilde{\varrho}_{1}],[x_{2},\tilde{\varrho}_{2}])\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}+\int q_{1}\tilde{\varrho}_{1}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}+\int q_{2}\tilde{\varrho}_{2}\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},[x_{2},1])\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime}+\int q_{1}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}+\int q_{2}\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},[x_{2},1])\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{2}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}+\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{2}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}+\int\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\int\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},[x_{2},1])\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime}+\int\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}(q_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}+\mu_{1}^{\prime\prime})\int\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},[x_{2},1])\,\,\mathrm{d}(q_{2}\tilde{\mu}_{2}^{\prime}+\mu_{2}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime}+\int\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\int\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},[x_{2},1])\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

∎

As an application of the previous properties, we compare the
formulation
3.1
of
Unbalanced Optimal Transport
via homogeneous couplings with
the formulation
based on the notion of semi-couplings
introduced by
[
Chi+18a
]
in a compact Euclidean setting.
We obtain a considerable extension
of the semi-coupling approach to
a general, possibly non-compact, setting.

Given
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
,
i=1,2
, we can consider the set

(3.22)

\hat{\Gamma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\Big\{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in(\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}))^{2}:\pi^{i}_{\sharp}(\gamma_{i})=\mu_{i},\ i=1,2\Big\}.

Every radially
1
-homogeneous cost function
\mathsf{H}
as
in (
3.2
)
induces a functional on
\hat{\Gamma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
given by

(3.23)

J_{\mathsf{H}}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}):=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}\Big(\Big[x_{1},\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\gamma_{1}}{\,\mathrm{d}\gamma}\Big],\Big[x_{2},\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\gamma_{2}}{\,\mathrm{d}\gamma}\Big]\Big)\,\,\mathrm{d}\gamma,\quad\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}),\ \gamma_{i}\ll\gamma.

Since
\mathsf{H}
is radially
1
-homogeneous,
(
3.23
)
does not depend on the choice of the dominating measure
\gamma
and one can consider the problem of minimizing
J_{\mathsf{H}}
in
\hat{\Gamma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):

(3.24)

\widehat{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\inf\Big\{J_{\mathsf{H}}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}):(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\hat{\Gamma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\Big\}.

When
\widehat{\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})<\infty
,
\mathsf{H}
satisfies (
3.2
) and it is also radially convex,
and
\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}
are compact,
it is not difficult to prove the
existence of an optimal pair
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\hat{\Gamma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
attaining the infimum in (
3.24
)
(see
[
Chi+18a
, Prop. 3.4]
).
The direct approach when
\mathsf{X}_{i}
are not compact is less clear, but existence of an optimal pair can be obtained as a consequence of the argument we are going to detail below in Theorem

3.11
.

Remark 3.10
.

The formulation (
3.24
) also allows for more general
(l.s.c., radially
1
-homogeneous and convex) functions
\hat{\mathsf{H}}

defined in

(\mathsf{X}_{1}\times[0,+\infty))\times(\mathsf{X}_{2}\times[0,+\infty))
which are not compatible with the equivalence relation
(
2.7
) inducing the
topological cone
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
.
This definition, however,
may not satisfy the invariance property stated in Remark
3.2
: consider, e.g., the case when

\mathsf{X}_{1}=\mathsf{X}_{2}=[0,a]
,
a>0
,

\mu_{1}=\delta_{0},

\mu_{2}=\bm{0}
,
and

\hat{\mathsf{H}}((x_{1},r_{1}),(x_{2},0))=r_{1}\mathrm{e}^{-|x_{1}-x_{2}|}
.
We clearly have

\widehat{\mathscr{U}_{\hat{\mathsf{H}}}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\mathrm{e}^{-a}
.
In order to have an intrinsic formulation,
we will assume that
\mathsf{H}
is compatible with the cone structure.

Theorem 3.11
.

If

\mathsf{H}
satisfies
(
3.2
)
then for every
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
non trivial
we have

(3.25)

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\leq\widehat{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

If, in addition to (
3.2
),
\mathsf{H}
is also radially convex,
then

(3.26)

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\widehat{\mathscr{U}}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

and the infimum in (
3.24
) is attained.

Proof.

We first observe that
every triple

(\gamma,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2})\times\hat{\Gamma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
with

\gamma_{i}\ll\gamma
induces a
homogeneous coupling
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
via the formula

(3.27)

\bm{\alpha}:=G_{\sharp}\gamma,\quad G(x_{1},x_{2}):=\Big(\big[x_{1},\varrho_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})\big],\big[x_{2},\varrho_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\big]\Big),\quad\varrho_{i}:=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\gamma_{i}}{\,\mathrm{d}\gamma},

such that

(3.28)

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=J_{\mathsf{H}}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}).

Therefore we immediately get
(
3.25
).

Let us now assume that
\mathsf{H}

is also radially convex and let
\bm{\alpha}

be a distinguished optimal coupling as in Theorem

3.9
.
Keeping the same notation of that Theorem
let us set
\mu_{i}(S_{i}^{\prime})=m_{i}>0

and let us set
\nu_{i}:=m_{i}^{-1}\mu_{i}^{\prime}.

We define

\gamma:=\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}+\mu_{1}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\nu_{2}+\nu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}^{\prime\prime}
,

\gamma_{1}:=\varrho_{1}\gamma^{\prime}+\mu_{1}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\nu_{2}
,

\gamma_{2}:=\varrho_{2}\gamma^{\prime}+\nu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}^{\prime\prime}
,
obtaining

\pi^{i}_{\sharp}\gamma_{i}=\mu_{i}^{\prime}+\mu_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\mu_{i}
so that

(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\hat{\Gamma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

Moreover

\displaystyle J_{\mathsf{H}}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})

\displaystyle=\int\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\varrho_{1}],[x_{2},\varrho_{2}])\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},0])\,\,\mathrm{d}(\mu_{1}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\nu_{2})+\int\mathsf{H}([x_{1},0],[x_{2},1])\,\,\mathrm{d}(\nu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).\qed

3.3.
The dual problem

In the next results we study the dual definition of the Unbalanced Optimal Transport cost
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
, for which we need the following definitions.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
be a function; we define the set of continuous functions

(3.29)

\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}:=\left\{(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\ :\begin{array}[]{l}\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\\
\text{for every }(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2},\,(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\end{array}\right\}

and, for every
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
, the functional
\mathcal{D}(\cdot;\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\mathcal{D}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2};\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2},\quad(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{2}).

Before stating the main duality result, let
us briefly recall
the Fenchel-Moreau Theorem in the
framework of a pair of vector spaces
E,F
placed in duality
by a nondegenerate bilinear map
\langle\cdot,\cdot,\rangle
,
see e.g.
[
ET87
]
.
We endow
E
with the weak topology

\sigma(E,F)
, the coarsest topology for which all
the functions
e\mapsto\langle e,f\rangle
,
f\in F
,
are continuous.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
\mathscr{F}:E\to(-\infty,+\infty]

be not identically
+\infty
and satisfying

(3.30)

\mathscr{F}(e)\geq\langle e,f\rangle-c\quad\text{for some $f\in F$, $c\in\mathbb{R}$ and every }e\in E.

The polar (or conjugate) function of

\mathscr{F}
is
the function
\mathscr{F}^{*}:F\to(-\infty,+\infty]

defined by

\mathscr{F}^{*}(f):=\sup_{e\in E}\langle e,f\rangle-\mathscr{F}(e)\quad\text{for every }f\in F.

Theorem 3.14

(Fenchel-Moreau)
.

Let
E
and
F
be vector spaces placed in duality by a nondegenerate bilinear map
\langle\cdot,\cdot,\rangle
and let
\mathscr{F}:E\to(-\infty,+\infty]

be satisfying (
3.30
) and not identically
+\infty
.
Then the lower semicontinuous (w.r.t. the topology
\sigma(E,F)
) and convex envelope of
\mathscr{F}

is given by the dual formula

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{F}\right)=\mathscr{F}^{**}(e):=\sup_{f\in F}\langle e,f\rangle-\mathscr{F}^{*}(f)\quad\text{for every }e\in E.

In particular,

\text{if $\mathscr{F}$ is convex and lower semicontinuous
then}\quad\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}^{**}.

The following is the main duality result and it gives also an independent proof of Theorem
3.5
.

Theorem 3.15

(Duality)
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be as in (
3.2
) and let
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}
and
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
be as in Definition
3.1
. Then

(3.31)

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\sup\left\{\mathcal{D}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2};\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}\right\}

for every
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
.

Proof.

Set
E:=\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
and
F:=\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
with the bilinear form

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:E\times F\to\mathbb{R},\quad\quad((\mu_{1},\mu_{2}),(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}))\mapsto\mathcal{D}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2};\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

This is a well defined nondegenerate bilinear form. We endow then
E
with the topology
\sigma(E,F)
which coincides exactly with the product weak topology.

Consider then the function
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}:E\mapsto(-\infty,+\infty]
defined as in Definition
3.1
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}^{*}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=\sup_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in E}\left\{\langle(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}),(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\rangle-\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})}\left\{\langle(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}),(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\rangle-\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x_{1},r_{1},x_{2},r_{2}}\left\{\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}-\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\right\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{ if }(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ elsewhere}\end{cases}.

Hence by Theorem
3.14
we have

(3.32)

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}^{**}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\sup\left\{\mathcal{D}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2};\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\mid(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}\right\}.

By Theorem
3.4
we know that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is convex and lower semicontinuous and stays below
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}
so that we clearly have
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}\leq\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)
. The other inequality is immediate: take any
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and any
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
; then

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\left((\varphi_{1}\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}+(\varphi_{2}\circ\mathsf{x}_{2})\mathsf{r}_{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}.

Passing to the supremum in
\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
and to the infimum in
\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and using (
3.32
), we conclude that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}\geq\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)
.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

If
\mathsf{H}
has the form

\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]):=\begin{cases}r_{1}\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2})\quad&\text{ if }r_{1}=r_{2}\geq 0\\
+\infty\quad&\text{ elsewhere }\end{cases},

for some
\mathsf{c}:\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}\to[0,+\infty]
lower semicontinuous function, we have that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$|$}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}_{2})}
is exactly the classical Optimal Transport cost induced by
\mathsf{c}
. This was indeed exploited in
[
SS22
]
.

In Definition
2.10
we have introduced the closed and convex envelope of
\mathsf{H}
which is obtained first convexifying
\mathsf{H}
“slice by slice” and then closing its graph globally in
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
. One could also compute first the closed convex envelope “slice by slice” and then glue together the resulting functions (see the definition of
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)
below). In the following result we show, making use of Theorem
3.15
, that the two procedures give raise to same object.

We also show that, even if
\mathsf{H}
is not convex, taking the closed and convex envelope of the singular cost
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}
is sufficient to also recover the Unbalanced Optimal Transport cost induced by the closed and convex envelope of
\mathsf{H}
.

Corollary 3.17
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be as in (
3.2
) and let
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)
be as in Definition
2.10
. Let us define
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right):\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
as

\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]):=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\right)(r_{1},r_{2})\quad\text{ for every }(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2},\,(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}.

Then

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])=\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])=\sup\left\{\varphi(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}:(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}\right\}

for every
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}
and every
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
. Moreover

(3.33)

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}\right)=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}=\mathscr{U}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}\text{ in }\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{X}_{2}).

Proof.

We denote by
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{H}}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
the restriction of
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
to
\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\Delta_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2})\cong\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
(see Lemma
2.4
).

It is clear that
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)\leq\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)\leq\mathsf{H}
so that

\mathscr{U}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}\leq\mathscr{U}_{\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}\leq\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}

and

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)=\mathsf{U}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}\leq\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)=\mathsf{U}_{\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}\leq\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{H}}\leq\mathsf{H},

where we used Theorem
3.4
and the radial convexity of
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)
and
\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)
.
Moreover, since
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is, lower semicontinuous, convex and stays below
\mathsf{H}
, we have that
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{H}}\leq\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)
. This gives that

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)=\mathsf{U}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}=\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)=\mathsf{U}_{\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}=\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{H}}

and in particular, using Theorem
3.15
, that

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])=\widehat{\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])=\sup\left\{\varphi(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}:(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}\right\}.

The fact that
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)=\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
gives that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}\leq\mathscr{S}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}
so that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}\leq\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}\right)
. However, by Theorem
3.4
, we
know that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)
so that
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)\leq\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}\right)
. Since, obviously, the other inequality
holds, we have
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}\right)
. Applying again
Theorem
3.4
to
\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)
we conclude that

\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{H}}\right)=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}\right)=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}=\mathscr{U}_{\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{H}\right)}.\qed

Remark 3.18
.

As an interesting application of the previous Corollary, we obtain a clarifying justification of two equivalent characterizations of the Hellinger-Kantorovich metric
(see (7.23) and (7.54) of
[
LMS18
]
) in a (complete, separable) metric space

(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{d})
, namely the formula

(3.34)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H\kern-3.0ptK}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

\displaystyle=\min_{\bm{\alpha}_{2}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\int\mathsf{d}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{2}=\min_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\int\mathsf{d}_{\pi/2,\mathfrak{C}}^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{2},

where
\mathsf{d}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is the canonical cone metric introduced in (
2.9
)
and

\mathsf{d}_{\pi/2,\mathfrak{C}}
is
the cone metric obtained by trucating the
argument of the
\cos
function at
\pi/2
:

(3.35)

\mathsf{d}_{\pi/2,\mathfrak{C}}([x,r],[y,s]):=\left(r^{2}+s^{2}-2rs\cos(\mathsf{d}(x,y)\wedge\pi/2)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad[x,r],[y,s]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}].

By Remark
2.13
,
the identity (
3.34
) is equivalent to

(3.36)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H\kern-3.0ptK}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

\displaystyle=\min_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\int\mathsf{d}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{2}\circ\mathsf{T}_{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=\min_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\int\mathsf{d}_{\pi/2,\mathfrak{C}}^{2}\circ\mathsf{T}_{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\beta}},

where
\mathsf{T}_{2}
is defined in (
2.14
);
it is then not difficult to check that

(3.37)

\mathsf{d}^{2}_{\pi/2,\mathfrak{C}}\circ\mathsf{T}_{2}=\operatorname{\overline{\mathrm{co}}}\left(\mathsf{d}^{2}_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ\mathsf{T}_{2}\right).

Therefore, the constant
\pi/2
in (
3.34
)
appears as a natural effect of the convexification of the cost function
\mathsf{d}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{2}\circ\mathsf{T}_{2}

and the identity (
3.33
).

3.4.
The Monge formulation

We define the Monge formulation of the Unbalanced Optimal Transport problem.

Definition 3.19

(Transport-growth maps and Monge formulation)
.

Given
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{1})
and Borel maps
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g}):\mathsf{X}_{1}\to\mathsf{X}_{2}\times[0,+\infty)
with
\mathsf{g}\in L^{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\mu_{1})
, we denote by
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}
the measure

(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}:=\mathsf{T}_{\sharp}(\mathsf{g}\mu_{1})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{2}).

We say that
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})
is a
transport-growth map
connecting
\mu_{1}
to
\mu_{2}
. If
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
is a proper Borel function, we define the Monge formulation of the Unbalanced Optimal Transport problem as

\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\inf\Big\{\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x,1],[\mathsf{T}(x),\mathsf{g}(x)])\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)\mid\mu_{2}=(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}\Big\}.

While in this section we only study the relation between the Kantorovich (i.e. the primal) and the Monge fofmulation of the Unbalanced Optimal Transport problem, in Theorem
5.13
we will provide sufficient conditions for the existence of an optimal transport-growth map realizing the infimum above. Clearly we have

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\leq\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}),\quad\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})

as a consequence of the fact that any transport-growth map
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})
induces an admissible
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
via the formula

\bm{\alpha}=([\text{id}_{\mathsf{X}_{1}},1],[\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g}])_{\sharp}\mu_{1}.

Recalling the definition
(
2.16
)
of the reduced cost function
\mathsf{h}
, we also have

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x,1],[\mathsf{T}(x),\mathsf{g}(x)])\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{h}(x,\mathsf{T}(x);\mathsf{g}(x))\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x).

We derive the main result of this subsection from the analogous one
[
Pra07
]
in the classical Optimal Transport theory. To do that we need the following definition.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0.+\infty]
be a proper and lower semicontinuous function. We define

\mathsf{OT}_{\mathsf{H}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}):=\min\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}:\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\right\},\quad\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}]),

where
\Gamma(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is the set of transport plans from
\alpha_{1}
to
\alpha_{2}
defined as

\Gamma(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}):=\left\{\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]):\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\bm{\gamma}=\alpha_{1},\,\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\bm{\gamma}=\alpha_{2}\right\}.

Notice that
\Gamma(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is not-empty if and only if
\alpha_{1}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}])=\alpha_{2}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}])
.
The following is an immediate consequence of
[
Pra07
, Proof of Theorem B]
.

Theorem 3.21
.

Let
\mathsf{X}_{i}
be Polish spaces, let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
be continuous and let
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}])
be such that
\alpha_{1}
is diffuse and
\alpha_{1}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}])>0
. Then for every
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
such that
\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}<+\infty
and every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a Borel map
\mathsf{F}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\to\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
such that

\mathsf{F}_{\sharp}\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2},\quad\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]}\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{y}_{2}))\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}+\varepsilon.

Choosing in particular an optimal
\bm{\gamma}
realizing the minimum in the definition of
\mathsf{OT}_{\mathsf{H}}
one can prove the equivalence between the Monge and Kantorovich formulations of the Optimal Transport problem. The connection between balanced and unbalanced Optimal Transport is of course given by the fact that if
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
for measures
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
, then
\bm{\alpha}\in\Gamma(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
, where
\alpha_{i}:=\pi^{i}_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha}
. A stronger connection actually holds, as reported in the following result which is the analogue of
[
LMS18
, Corollary 7.7, Corollary 7.13]
;
the proof of the second statement
is identical and thus omitted,
the proof of the first statement follows immediately by Lemma

2.12
.

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be as in (
3.2
). Then for every
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
with
R=R(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
as in (
2.13
), it holds

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\min\left\{\mathsf{OT}_{\mathsf{H}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}):\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{i}])\,,\,\mathfrak{h}^{1}(\alpha_{i})=\mu_{i},\,\,i=1,2\right\}.

In particular we have
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\mathsf{OT}_{\mathsf{H}}(\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha},\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha})
for every optimal
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
(cf. (
3.5
)).
If
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{X}_{1}=\mathsf{X}_{2}
and
(\mu_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
with
N\geq 2
, then there exist
(\alpha_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}])
such that

\mathfrak{h}^{1}(\alpha_{i})=\mu_{i},\quad\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{i-1},\mu_{i})=\mathsf{OT}_{\mathsf{H}}(\alpha_{i-1},\alpha_{i})\quad\text{ for every }i\in\{2,\dots,N\}.

We first show that the cost of any coupling
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
,
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
, can be approximated by the cost of couplings with the same homogeneous marginals not charging the vertex of the first cone.

Proposition 3.23
.

Let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
be a proper, radially
1
-homogeneous and continuous function, and let
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
be such that
\mu_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1})>0
.
Then for every
\varepsilon>0
and every
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
such that
\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}<+\infty
there exists
\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
with
\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}(\{\mathfrak{y}_{1}=\mathfrak{o}_{1}\})=0
such that

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}+\varepsilon.

Proof.

We define the measures

\bm{\alpha}^{tg}:=\bm{\alpha}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\{\mathfrak{y}_{1}\neq\mathfrak{o}_{1}\},\quad\bm{\alpha}^{o}:=\bm{\alpha}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\{\mathfrak{y}_{1}=\mathfrak{o}_{1}\},\quad\mu_{2}^{tg}:=\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{2}(\bm{\alpha}^{tg}),\quad\mu_{2}^{o}:=\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{2}(\bm{\alpha}^{o});

it is not restrictive to assume that
\mu_{2}^{o}(\mathsf{X}_{2})>0
so that
\bm{\alpha}^{o}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])>0
. For a bounded Borel map
\vartheta:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to(0,\infty)
and a measure

{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])

we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{prd}_{\vartheta,2}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}):={}

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}/\vartheta(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})),\quad\mathfrak{y}_{i}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}],

\displaystyle\dil_{\vartheta,2}({\bm{\beta}}):={}

\displaystyle(\mathrm{prd}_{\vartheta,2})_{\sharp}(\vartheta\,{\bm{\beta}}).

Notice that
\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{2}(\dil_{\vartheta,2}({\bm{\beta}}))=\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{2}({\bm{\beta}})

and

(3.38)

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\,\mathrm{d}\big(\dil_{\vartheta,2}({\bm{\beta}})\big)=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}(\vartheta(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\beta}}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}).

We fix
\varepsilon>0
and we set

\displaystyle\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}):=\sup\Big\{

\displaystyle\lambda\in[0,1/2]:\mathsf{H}(r\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\leq\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{o}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})+\varepsilon_{0}\text{ for every }r\in[0,\lambda]\Big\},

\displaystyle\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}):=\sup\Big\{

\displaystyle\lambda\in[0,1/2]:\mathsf{H}(r\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\leq\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})+\varepsilon_{0}\text{ for every }r\in[1-\lambda,1]\Big\},

where
\varepsilon_{0}
is given by

\varepsilon_{0}:=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\varepsilon}{\mu_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1})+\bm{\alpha}^{tg}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])}.

Since
\mathsf{H}
is continuous, it is not difficult to check that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}

define strictly positive upper semicontinuous maps (thus Borel)
from
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
to
(0,1/2].
Now we set
c:=\mu_{2}^{o}(\mathsf{X}_{2})/\mu_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1})
and we consider any
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},c^{-1}\mu_{2}^{o})
.
We “lift”
\bm{\gamma}
to a plan

{\bm{\beta}}:=\ell_{\sharp}\bm{\gamma}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}^{o})

using the map

\ell(x_{1},x_{2}):=([x_{1},1],[x_{2},c])
and we consider the rescaled plan
{\bm{\beta}}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
and its first homogeneous marginal

{\bm{\beta}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\dil_{\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},2}({\bm{\beta}}),\quad\mu_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{1}({\bm{\beta}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}).

Since
\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\leq 1/2

we easily get

\mu_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}\ll\mu_{1}
and we call

\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}
the Lebesgue density of
\mu_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}
with respect to
\mu_{1}
, i.e.

(3.39)

\mu_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mu_{1},\quad\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}\quad\text{is a Borel map with values in $(0,1/2]$}.

In a similar way we
define the homogeneous plan

{\bm{\beta}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}
and its first homogeneous marginal

{\bm{\beta}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}:=\dil_{(1-\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}),2}(\bm{\alpha}^{tg}),\quad\mu_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{1}({\bm{\beta}}^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}),\quad\mu_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}=\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\mu_{1}.

We can select a Borel representative of
\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}

such that

(1-\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})

takes values in
(0,1/2]
.
We eventually consider

(3.40)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\varepsilon}:=\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}\land(1-\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}),\quad\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}:={}

\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}{\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\quad\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}:={}

\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}{1-\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}},

(3.41)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}:={}

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\quad\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}:={}

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(1-\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})+(1-\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}),

(3.42)

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{o}:={}

\displaystyle\dil_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},2}({\bm{\beta}}),

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{tg}:={}

\displaystyle\dil_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime},2}(\bm{\alpha}^{tg}).

Since the
\dil_{\cdot,2}

dialations preserve the second homogeneous marginals,
we easily get

(3.43)

\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{2}(\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{o})=\mu_{2}^{o},\quad\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{2}(\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{tg})=\mu_{2}^{tg}.

Concerning the first marginals, for every test function
\zeta\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{1})
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\zeta\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{o}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\zeta\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\,\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\beta}}=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\zeta\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\,\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\zeta\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\zeta\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\zeta\rho_{\varepsilon}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1},

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\zeta\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{tg}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\zeta\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{tg}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\zeta\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(1-\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{tg}+\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\zeta\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}(1-\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{tg}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\zeta\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\,\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\beta}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\zeta(1-\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\zeta\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\zeta(1-\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\zeta\Big(\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\varrho_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}+(1-\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})\Big)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\zeta(1-\varrho_{\varepsilon})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}

so that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{1}(\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{o})

\displaystyle=\varrho_{\varepsilon}\mu_{1},\quad\mathfrak{h}^{1}_{1}(\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{tg})=(1-\varrho_{\varepsilon})\mu_{1}.

We deduce that the plan

\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}:=\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{tg}+\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{o}\quad\text{belongs to}\quad\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

By linearity, the
\mathsf{H}
-cost associated with
\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}

is the sum of the corresponding costs
associated with
\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{tg}
and

\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{o}
;
using (
3.38
)
and observing that

0<\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\leq\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
,

1-\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\leq\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\leq 1
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{o}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\big(\sigma^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\beta}}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\Big(\mathsf{H}\big(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}\Big)+\varepsilon_{0}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}{\bm{\beta}}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{o}+\varepsilon_{0}\mu_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{o}+\varepsilon/2,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}^{tg}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{tg}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon)\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{tg}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{tg}+\varepsilon\bm{\alpha}^{tg}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}^{tg}+\varepsilon/2.\qed

Theorem 3.24
.

Let
\mathsf{X}_{i}
be Polish spaces, let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
be a proper, radially
1
-homogeneous and continuous function, and let
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
be such that
\mu_{1}
is a diffuse measure and
\mu_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1})>0
.
Then for every
\varepsilon>0
and every
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
such that
\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}<+\infty
there exists a transport-growth map
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})
connecting
\mu_{1}
to
\mu_{2}
such that

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[\mathsf{T}(x_{1}),\mathsf{g}(x_{1})])\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{1})\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}+\varepsilon.

In particular

\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\mathscr{M}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

Proof.

Let
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
with
\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}<+\infty
and
\varepsilon>0
be fixed. By Proposition
3.23
we can find
\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
with
\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}(\{\mathfrak{y}_{1}=\mathfrak{o}_{1}\})=0
such that

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}+\varepsilon/2.

Using Lemma
2.8
applied to
\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}
and
\vartheta
defined as

\vartheta([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])=\begin{cases}r_{1}\quad&\text{ if }r_{1}>0,\\
1\quad&\text{ if }r_{1}=0,\end{cases}

we can assume that
\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}
is concentrated on
\{\mathsf{r}_{1}=1\}
. Since
\mu_{1}
is non atomic, we deduce that also
\alpha_{\varepsilon}^{1}:=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}
is non atomic so that, also observing that
\mathsf{H}
is continuous, we can find thanks to Theorem
3.21
a Borel map
\mathsf{F}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\to\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
such that
\mathsf{F}_{\sharp}\alpha_{\varepsilon}^{1}=\alpha_{\varepsilon}^{2}:=\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}
and

(3.44)

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]}\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}))\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\varepsilon}^{1}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})-\varepsilon/2\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}.

We define the Borel maps

\mathsf{T}(x_{1}):=(\mathsf{x}\circ\mathsf{F})([x_{1},1]),\quad\mathsf{g}(x_{1}):=(\mathsf{r}\circ\mathsf{F})([x_{1},1]),\quad x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1},

and we notice that
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
and

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]}\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}))\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\varepsilon}^{1}(\mathfrak{y}_{1})=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[\mathsf{T}(x_{1}),\mathsf{g}(x_{1})])\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{1}).

This concludes the proof.
∎

4.
Existence of a maximazing pair for the dual problem

In this section we provide sufficent conditions for the existence of a maximizing pair
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
(see Definition
3.12
) i.e. such that
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathsf{X}_{2})
and

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

\displaystyle\text{ for every }([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}],

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

A somehow complementary set of assumptions for which the same conclusion holds is presented in Appendix
B
. The following result is a simple consequence of compactness.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
(\mathsf{X}_{i},\mathsf{d}_{i})
be compact metric spaces and assume that
\Omega\subset\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}
is an open set such that
\pi^{i}(\Omega)=\mathsf{X}_{i}
,
i=1,2
. Then there exists a finite set
\mathcal{U}:=\{x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n},r_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}\subset\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}\times(0,+\infty)
such that

(4.1)

\bigcup_{n}B_{r_{n}}(x_{i}^{n})=\mathsf{X}_{i},\quad i=1,2\quad\quad\bigcup_{n}\overline{B_{r_{n}}(x_{1}^{n})}\times\overline{B_{r_{n}}(x_{2}^{n})}\subset\Omega.

If
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
is such that
\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)=\mathsf{X}_{i}
, then

(4.2)

m(\mathcal{U},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\min_{i=1,2}\min_{n=1,\dots,N}\mu_{i}\left(B_{r_{n}}(x_{i}^{n})\right)>0.

In order to use this result, in this section, we are going to assume without mentioning it again that
\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
\mathsf{X}_{2}
are compact and metrizable spaces. We also assume that

(4.3)

\begin{split}\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty)\text{ is continuous, radially $1$-homogeneous and convex }\\
\text{and that there exists an open set }\Omega_{\mathsf{H}}\subset\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}\text{ with }\pi^{i}(\Omega_{\mathsf{H}})=\mathsf{X}_{i}\text{ such that }\\
\lim_{r_{1}\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},1])-\mathsf{H}([\mathfrak{0}_{1},[x_{2},1])}{r_{1}}=-\infty\quad\text{ for every }(x_{1},x_{2})\in\Omega_{\mathsf{H}},\\
\lim_{r_{2}\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}])-\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],\mathfrak{0}_{2})}{r_{2}}=-\infty\quad\text{ for every }(x_{1},x_{2})\in\Omega_{\mathsf{H}}.\end{split}

To simplify the notation, we set

\mathsf{H}_{1}(x_{1}):=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],\mathfrak{o}_{2}),\quad\mathsf{H}_{2}(x_{2}):=\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{o}_{1},[x_{2},1])\quad x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1},\,x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}

and

(4.4)

\kappa_{1}:=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}<+\infty,\quad\kappa_{2}:=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}<+\infty.

Clearly
\mathsf{H}\leq\mathsf{H}_{1}+\mathsf{H}_{2}
; we remark that the meaning of (
4.3
) is to impose a control on the derivatives of
\mathsf{H}
at the boundary of
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
for a sufficiently large set of points
(x_{1},x_{2})
.

Example 4.2
.

Both the functions in (
2.19
) and (
2.18
) satisfy (
4.3
) with
\Omega_{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{GHK}}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\Omega_{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{HK}}}=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\pi/2\}
.

We start with a few preliminary lemmas that provide bounds on pairs in
\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Assume that
\mathsf{H}
is as in (
4.3
) and let
\mathcal{U}=\{x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n},r_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}
be as in Lemma
4.1
for distances
\mathsf{d}_{i}
metrizing
\mathsf{X}_{i}
,
i=1,2
and
\Omega=\Omega_{\mathsf{H}}
. If
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
are such that
\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)=\mathsf{X}_{i}
, then any pair
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
such that

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}\geq 0

satisfies also

\max_{\overline{B_{r_{n}}(x_{i}^{n})}}\varphi_{i}\geq-\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+1}{m(\mathcal{U},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\quad\text{ for every }n\in\{1,\dots,N\}\text{ and }i=1,2,

where
\kappa_{i}
are as in (
4.4
) and
m(\mathcal{U},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is as in (
4.2
).

Proof.

We claim that

(4.5)

\int_{B_{r_{i}}(x_{1}^{n})}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}\geq-(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+1),\quad\int_{B_{r_{i}}(x_{2}^{n})}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}\geq-(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+1)\quad n=1,\dots,N.

Indeed, if there exists
i\in\{1,2\}
(say
i=1
) and
n\in\{1,\dots,N\}
such that

\int_{B_{r_{n}}(x_{1}^{n})}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}<-(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+1),

then

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}=\int_{B_{r_{n}}(x_{1}^{n})}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}\setminus B_{r_{n}}(x_{1}^{n})}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}<-(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{1}+1)+\kappa_{1}=-(\kappa_{2}+1).

Thus

\kappa_{2}\geq\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}\geq-\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}\geq\kappa_{2}+1,

a contradiction. By (
4.5
) we have, for every
i=1,2
and
n=1,\dots,N
, that

\mu_{i}\left(B_{r_{n}}(x_{i}^{n})\right)\sup_{B_{r_{n}}(x_{i}^{n})}\varphi_{i}\geq\int_{B_{r_{n}}(x_{i}^{n})}\varphi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}\geq-(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+1),

hence

\sup_{B_{r_{n}}(x_{i}^{n})}\varphi_{i}\geq-\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+1}{m(\mathcal{U},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}.

∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Assume that
\mathsf{H}
is as in (
4.3
) and that
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
are such that
\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)=\mathsf{X}_{i}
. Then there exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that any pair
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
such that

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}\geq 0

satisfies also

\varphi_{i}(x_{i})\leq\mathsf{H}_{i}(x_{i})-\varepsilon\quad\text{ for every }x_{i}\in\mathsf{X}_{i},\quad i=1,2.

Proof.

We prove the statement for
i=1
, being the other case completely analogous. Suppose by contradiction that there exists
(\varphi_{1}^{j},\varphi_{2}^{j})_{j}\subset\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
with
\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}^{j}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}^{j}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}\geq 0
and
(z_{j})_{j}\subset\mathsf{X}_{1}
such that

\mathsf{H}_{1}(z_{j})-\varphi_{1}^{j}(z_{j})\to 0

as
j\to+\infty
. Up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that

0\leq\mathsf{H}_{1}(z_{j})-\varphi_{1}^{j}(z_{j})\leq\frac{1}{j}\quad\text{ for every }j\in\mathbb{N}

and, by compactness of
\mathsf{X}_{1}
, the existence of
z\in\mathsf{X}_{1}
such that
z_{j}\to z
. Let
\mathcal{U}=\{x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n},r_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}
be as in Lemma
4.1
for distances
\mathsf{d}_{i}
metrizing
\mathsf{X}_{i}
,
i=1,2
and
\Omega=\Omega_{\mathsf{H}}
; let

C:=\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+1}{m(\mathcal{U},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}.

Since
z\in B_{r_{n}}(x_{1}^{n})
(see (
4.1
)) for some
n\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, we can assume, up to passing again to a subsequence, that
z_{j}\in B_{r_{n}}(x_{1}^{n})
for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
. By Lemma
4.3
, we can find,
y_{j}\in\overline{B_{r_{n}}(x_{2}^{n})}
such that
\varphi_{2}^{j}(y_{j})\geq-C
. By compactness of
\mathsf{X}_{2}
, we can assume that
y_{j}\to y\in\overline{B_{r_{n}}(x_{2}^{n})}
. We have thus proven the existence of
(z_{j},y_{j})\in\Omega
such that
(z_{j},y_{j})\to(z,y)\in\Omega
with

0\leq\mathsf{H}_{1}(z_{j})-\varphi_{1}^{j}(z_{j})\leq\frac{1}{j},\quad\varphi_{2}^{j}(y_{j})\geq-C\quad\text{ for every }j\in\mathbb{N}.

We have

r_{1}\left(\mathsf{H}_{1}(z_{j})-\frac{1}{j}\right)-Cr_{2}\leq\varphi_{1}^{j}(z_{j})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}^{j}(y_{j})r_{2}\leq\mathsf{H}([z_{j},r_{1}],[y_{j},r_{2}])\quad\text{ for every }r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0.

Choosing
r_{1}=1
, we get

\frac{\mathsf{H}([z_{j},1],[y_{j},r_{2}]-\mathsf{H}_{1}(z_{j})}{r_{2}}\geq-C-\frac{1}{jr_{2}}\quad\text{ for every }j\in\mathbb{N},\,r_{2}>0.

Passing first to the limit as
j\to+\infty
and then to the limit as
r_{2}\downarrow 0
, we obtain

\lim_{r_{2}\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathsf{H}([z,1],[y,r_{2}]-\mathsf{H}_{1}(x_{\infty})}{r_{2}}\geq-C>-\infty,

a contradiction with (
4.3
).
∎

The following definition is simply the analogue of the classical
\mathsf{c}
-transform (see e.g.
[
AGS08
, Definition 6.1.2]
).

Definition 4.5
.

Let
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
. We define the Borel functions
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}:\mathsf{X}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}:\mathsf{X}_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2})

\displaystyle:=\inf_{x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}}\inf_{\alpha\geq 0}\biggl\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2},1])-\alpha\varphi_{1}(x_{1})\biggr\},\quad x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2},

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle:=\inf_{x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}}\inf_{\alpha\geq 0}\biggl\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},\alpha])-\alpha\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2})\biggr\},\quad x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}.

Using the previous lemmas, in the following proposition we prove that the
\mathsf{H}
-transform of a pair in
\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
can be computed restricting the minimization to a compact set. As a consequence, we obtain uniform bounds and uniform continuity for the admissible pair, as it happens in the classical case (see e.g. the discussion after
[
San15
, Definition 1.10]
).

Proposition 4.6
.

Assume that
\mathsf{H}
is as in (
4.3
) and that
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
are such that
\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)=\mathsf{X}_{i}
. Then there exists constants
R>1
and
M>0
such that, if
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
are such that

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}\geq 0,

then
\|\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}\|_{\infty},\|\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}\|_{\infty}\leq M
and

(4.6)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=\inf_{x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}}\inf_{0\leq\alpha\leq R}\biggl\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2},1])-\alpha\varphi_{1}(x_{1})\biggr\},\quad x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2},

(4.7)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=\inf_{x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}}\inf_{0\leq\alpha\leq R}\biggl\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},\alpha])-\alpha\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2})\biggr\},\quad x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}.

In particular,
(\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}},\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
,
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}\geq\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}\geq\varphi_{2}
. Finally, if
\mathsf{d}_{i}
are distances metrizing
\mathsf{X}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, then
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}
is
\mathsf{d}_{1}
-uniformly continuous and
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}
is
\mathsf{d}_{2}
-uniformly continuous, both with the same (uniform)
\mathsf{d}_{1}\otimes_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathsf{d}_{2}
-modulus of continuity of
\mathsf{H}
on
\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
(cf. (
2.10
)).

Proof.

Let
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
be as in the statement. By Lemma
4.4
we know that there exists
\varepsilon>0
(not depending on the pair) such that

\varphi_{1}(x_{1})\leq\mathsf{H}_{1}(x_{1})-\varepsilon\quad\text{ for every }x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}.

Then, by uniform continuity of
\mathsf{H}
on
\mathfrak{C}_{1}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
, we can find
0<\delta<1
such that

\left|\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}])-\mathsf{H}_{1}(x_{1})\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\quad\text{ for every }0\leq r_{2}\leq\delta.

If we define

R:=1+\frac{1}{\delta}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\left(\max_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}_{2}+\max_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}_{1}+1\right)
,
then, for every
\alpha>R
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2},1])-\alpha\varphi_{1}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2},1])-\mathsf{H}_{1}(x_{1})\alpha+\alpha\left(\mathsf{H}_{1}(x_{1})-\varphi_{1}(x_{1})\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha\left(\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},1/\alpha])-\mathsf{H}_{1}(x_{1})\right)+\alpha\left(\mathsf{H}_{1}(x_{1})-\varphi_{1}(x_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\alpha\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{H}_{2}(x_{2})+1.

Thus, for every
x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}
, we get

\displaystyle\inf_{x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}}\inf_{\alpha>R}\left\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2},1])-\alpha\varphi_{1}(x_{1})\right\}
