\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{H}_{2}(x_{2})+1

\displaystyle>\inf_{x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}}\inf_{0\leq\alpha\leq R}\left\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2},1])-\alpha\varphi_{1}(x_{1})\right\}

and this proves (
4.6
). The proof of (
4.7
) is analogous.

The fact that
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}\geq\varphi_{2}
,
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}\geq\varphi_{1}
and

\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2})r_{2}\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{ for every }(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2},\,r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0

follows by the definition of
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}
and
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}
. It is then clear that
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}
(resp.
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}
) is bounded from below by
\min_{x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}
(resp.
\min_{x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1})
and by above by
\max_{x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}_{2}
(resp.
\max_{x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}_{1}
). Let
\mathsf{d}_{i}
be distances metrizing
\mathsf{X}_{i}
,
i=1,2
; let now
x_{2},x_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}
, then, recalling (
2.11
), we have

(4.8)

\begin{split}\left|\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2})-\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2}^{\prime})\right|&\leq\sup_{x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}}\sup_{0\leq\alpha\leq R}\left|\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2},1])-\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2}^{\prime},1])\right|\\
&\leq\omega_{\mathsf{H}}^{R}\left((\mathsf{d}_{1}\otimes_{\mathfrak{C}}\mathsf{d}_{2})\left(([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2},1]),([x_{1},\alpha],[x_{2}^{\prime},1])\right)\right)\\
&=\omega_{\mathsf{H}}^{R}(\mathsf{d}_{2,\mathfrak{C}}([x_{2},1],[x_{2}^{\prime},1]))\\
&\leq\omega_{\mathsf{H}}^{R}(\mathsf{d}_{2}(x_{2},x_{2}^{\prime})),\end{split}

where
\omega_{\mathsf{H}}^{R}
is the (uniform) modulus of continuity of
\mathsf{H}
on
\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
and we have used that
\mathsf{d}_{2,\mathfrak{C}}([x_{2},1],[x_{2}^{\prime},1]))\leq\mathsf{d}_{2}(x_{2},x_{2}^{\prime})
(see (
2.9
), (
2.11
) and formula (7.5) in
[
LMS18
]
). The analogous statement for
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}
follows by the same strategy. This proves that
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}
and
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}
are uniformly continuous with the same (uniform) modulus of continuity of
\mathsf{H}
on
\mathfrak{C}_{R}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
and concludes the proof that
(\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}},\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
. Let
\mathcal{U}=\{x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n},r_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}
be as in Lemma
4.1
for the distances
\mathsf{d}_{i}
and
\Omega=\Omega_{\mathsf{H}}
; we define (recalling Lemma
4.3
)

M:=\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+1}{m(\mathcal{U},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\omega_{\mathsf{H}}^{R}(\diam{\mathsf{X}_{1}})+\omega_{\mathsf{H}}^{R}(\diam{\mathsf{X}_{2}})+\max_{x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}_{1}+\max_{x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}_{2},

we have that
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}\leq\mathsf{H}_{2}\leq M
and, by (
4.8
), we get

\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2})\geq\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}(x_{2}^{\prime})-\omega_{\mathsf{H}}^{R}(\mathsf{d}_{2}(x_{2},x_{2}^{\prime}))\geq-\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+1}{m(\mathcal{U},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\omega_{\mathsf{H}}^{R}(\diam{\mathsf{X}_{2}})\geq-M\quad\text{ for every }x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2},

where
x_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}
is some point where
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}}
is larger than
-\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+1}{m(\mathcal{U},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}
(whose existence is given by Lemma
4.3
). The proof for
\varphi_{1}^{\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}
is the same.
∎

With the result of Proposition
4.6
it is straightforward to obtain the existence of a maximizing pair.

Theorem 4.7

(Existence of optimal continuous potentials)
.

Assume that
\mathsf{H}
is as in (
4.3
) and that
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
are such that
\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)=\mathsf{X}_{i}
. Then there exists
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
such that

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

Proof.

If
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=0
, we can take
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
to be the null functions. We thus assume that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})>0
. If this is the case, we can find a maximizing sequence
(\varphi_{1}^{j},\varphi_{2}^{j})_{j}\subset\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
for the dual problem (
3.31
) with

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}^{j}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}^{j}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}\geq 0\quad\text{ for every }j\in\mathbb{N}.

If we consider distances
\mathsf{d}_{i}
metrizing
\mathsf{X}_{i}
, by Proposition
4.6
we have that
(\varphi_{1}^{j,\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}},\varphi_{1}^{j,\mathsf{H}})_{j}\subset\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
is a maximizing sequence of equi-uniformly continuous and equi-bounded functions. By Arzelà–Ascoli theorem, we can assume, up to passing to a subsequence, that there exists a pair
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
such that
(\varphi_{1}^{j,\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}},\varphi_{1}^{j,\mathsf{H}})\to(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
uniformly on the compact space
\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}
. By dominated convergence, we have

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}=\lim_{j}\left(\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}^{j,\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{1}^{j,\mathsf{H}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}\right)=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).\qed

5.
Optimality conditions

In this section we provide sufficient and necessary conditions for a plan
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
to be optimal. In the following
\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
\mathsf{X}_{2}
are completely regular spaces and we will often assume that

(5.1)

\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]\text{ is a proper, radially $1$-homogeneous, convex and l.s.c.\penalty\ function}.

The cyclical monotonicity of the support of an admissible plan plays a crucial role also in the unbalanced setting.
We recall here the important definition of
cyclical monotonicity of a given set with respect to
a cost
\mathsf{H}
.

Definition 5.1

(
\mathsf{H}
-cyclical monotonicity)
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
and let
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
; we say that
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone if for every finite family of points
\{(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset\Gamma
and every permutation
\sigma
of
\{1,\dots,N\}
it holds

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{\sigma(i)}).

The following result shows that optimal
1
-homogeneous couplings
are concentrated on a
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone
set
\Gamma

which is also
a radial convex cone
.
It extends
to the unbalanced optimal transport setting
the classical
[
AGS08
, Necessity part of Theorem 6.14]
.

Proposition 5.2

(Necessity of cyclical
monotonicity)
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be as in (
5.1
),
let
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
for
i=1,2
, let
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
be optimal and suppose that
\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}<+\infty
. Then
\bm{\alpha}
is concentrated on a
\sigma
-compact (thus Borel)
radial convex cone
\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
which is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically
monotone.

Proof.

Let
\{(\varphi_{1}^{k},\varphi_{2}^{k})\}_{k\geq 1}\subset\Phi_{\mathsf{H}}
be a maximizing sequence for the dual problem (
3.31
) and let us define

\mathsf{H}_{k}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]):=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])-\varphi_{1}^{k}(x_{1})r_{1}-\varphi_{2}^{k}(x_{2})r_{2},\quad([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}].

Since

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int\mathsf{H}_{k}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\,\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}=0,

there exist a subsequence
m\mapsto k(m)
and a
\sigma
-compact (thus Borel) subset
G\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
on which
\bm{\alpha}
is concentrated
s.t.
\mathsf{H}_{k(m)}\to 0
on
G
as
m\to+\infty
. Since

\mathsf{H}_{k(m)}
is a radially convex and
1
-homogeneous function,
this convergence takes place also on the
radial convex cone
\Gamma:=\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(G\right)\right)
generated by
G
:

\Gamma:=\left\{\Big(\big[x_{1},\sum_{i=0}^{2}\lambda_{i}r_{1}^{i}],[x_{2},\sum_{i=0}^{2}\lambda_{i}r_{2}^{i}])\mid([x_{1},r_{1}^{i}],[x_{2},r_{2}^{i}])\in\Gamma,\ \lambda_{i}\geq 0\Big)\right\}.

Writing
G
as a countable union of an increasing sequence
of compact sets
K_{n}\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
,
it is easy to see that
\Gamma=\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\hat{K}_{n}
, where

\hat{K}_{n}:=\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(K_{n}\right)\right)
and it is not restrictive to assume that

(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})\in K_{n}
. Since each
\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(K_{n}\right)
is clearly compact in
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]

and
\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(K_{n}\right)\right)=\cup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\big\{(m\mathfrak{y}_{1},m\mathfrak{y}_{2}):(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(K_{n}\right)\big\}
is the union of a countable family of compact
sets,
we conclude that
\Gamma
is
\sigma
-compact as well.
Let now
\{(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset\Gamma
be a
finite family of points and let
\sigma
be a permutation of
\{1,\dots,N\}
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{\sigma(i)})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(r_{1}^{i}\varphi_{1}^{k(m)}(x_{1}^{i})+r_{2}^{\sigma(i)}\varphi_{2}^{k(m)}(x_{2}^{\sigma(i)})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(r_{1}^{i}\varphi_{1}^{k(m)}(x_{1}^{i})+r_{2}^{i}\varphi_{2}^{k(m)}(x_{2}^{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{i})-\mathsf{H}_{k(m)}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{i})\right).

Letting
m\to+\infty
, we obtain the sought
\mathsf{H}
-cyclical
monotonicity of
\Gamma
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

The previous proof shows that
the radial convex cone
\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(G\right)\right)
generated by a
\sigma
-compact set

G\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]

is
\sigma
-compact.
In particular, for a finite Radon measure
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}])

the following properties are equivalent:

(1)

\bm{\alpha}
is concentrated on a Borel set
G
such that
the generated radial convex cone
\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{co}}\left(G\right)\right)
is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically
monotone;

(2)

\bm{\alpha}
is concentrated on a
\sigma
-compact radial convex
cone
\Gamma
which is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically
monotone.

We devote the remaining part of this section to formulate a converse
statement to Proposition
5.2
.
We first introduce a few notions
related to the natural directed graph structures
induced by
\mathsf{H}
and subsets
\Gamma
of

\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
. A similar approach has already been considered when dealing with optimality conditions for possibly infinite costs in the classical Optimal Transport theory
[
Bei+09
,
BC10
]
.

5.1.
Simple directed graphs and oriented walks

Recall that a directed graph
\mathcal{G}
in a set
\mathsf{Z}
is an ordered
pair
(V,\mathcal{A})
where
V\subset\mathsf{Z}
and
\mathcal{A}
is a set of ordered pairs in

V\times V
.
A (oriented)
\mathcal{A}
-walk
P
in
V

is just a sequence of
elements
(\mathfrak{y}^{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{y}^{N})
in
V^{N+1}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, such that
each pair of consecutive elements
(\mathfrak{y}^{h-1},\mathfrak{y}^{h})
belongs to

\mathcal{A}
,
h=1,\cdots,N
.
We denote by
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{y}^{\prime\prime}|V,\mathcal{A})
the
collections of
\mathcal{A}
-walks in
V
whose first
and last elements are
\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{y}^{\prime\prime}
respectively.
We will omit to write
V
when
V=\mathsf{Z}
and we will also omit to
write
\mathcal{A}
if the set of arcs is clear from the context.
When
\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{y}^{\prime\prime}
then we say that
\mathcal{P}
is a cycle.

If
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}^{1}\to{\mathfrak{y}}^{2})
and

P^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}^{2}\to{\mathfrak{y}}^{3})

we can construct a new walk
P=P^{\prime}+P^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}^{1}\to{\mathfrak{y}}^{3})
by joining
P^{\prime}
with
P^{\prime\prime}
.

We say that a (ordered) pair of points
(\mathfrak{y}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathsf{Z}

is
\mathcal{A}
-connected if
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{y}^{\prime\prime})
is not empty.
A directed graph
(V,\mathcal{A})
is connected if every pair of
points in
V
is
\mathcal{A}
-connected.

Given a function
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]

and a set

\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]
,
we construct a directed (bipartite) graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}

whose vertices belong to

\mathsf{Z}=\mathsf{Z}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]:=\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\sqcup\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}],\quad\text{the disjoint union of
$\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]$ and $\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]$.}

We first consider the set of
arcs
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}}
consisting of all
the (ordered) pairs
(\mathfrak{y}_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1})\in\mathsf{Z}\times\mathsf{Z}
such that
\mathfrak{y}_{i}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}]
and

\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<+\infty
.
We can also easily identify
\Gamma
with a set of arcs, i.e. all
the (ordered) pairs
(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\mathsf{Z}\times\mathsf{Z}
such that

(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\Gamma

(notice that
\Gamma
can be canonically identified
with a subset of
\mathsf{Z}\times\mathsf{Z}
).

We eventually set

\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}:=\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}}\cup\Gamma.

When
\mathsf{H}
is the null function
0
(or any finite function)
we have
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]
and

\mathcal{A}_{0,\Gamma}=\big(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\big)\cup\Gamma.

Recalling that
\pi^{i}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}]
are the canonical
projections, we also consider the subsets

(5.2)

\Gamma_{i}:=\pi^{i}(\Gamma)\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}],\quad V_{\Gamma}:={\Gamma_{1}}\sqcup{\Gamma_{2}}\subset\mathsf{Z};

V_{\Gamma}
is the collection of all the
vertices obtained by applying to
\Gamma

the two projections on

\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}]
,
i=1,2
, canonically identified with the corresponding
subsets of
\mathsf{Z}
. We set

\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}:=\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}\cap(V_{\Gamma}\times V_{\Gamma}).

Notice that if the initial and final points
\mathfrak{y}^{0},\mathfrak{y}^{N}
of a

\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk
P
belong to

\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]
we will have

(5.3)

\begin{gathered}N=2n\text{ even},\quad\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{k}:=\mathfrak{y}^{2k}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\quad\text{for $0\leq k\leq n$,}\quad\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{k}:=\mathfrak{y}^{2k+1}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\quad\text{for $0\leq k<n$},\\
(\mathfrak{y}^{2k},\mathfrak{y}^{2k+1})=(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{k},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{k})\in\Gamma,\quad\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}^{2k+2},\mathfrak{y}^{2k+1})=\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{k+1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{k})<+\infty,\\
{\mathfrak{y}}^{2k}={\mathfrak{y}}_{1}^{k}\in{\Gamma_{1}},\ {\mathfrak{y}}^{2k+1}={\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{k}\in{\Gamma_{2}}\quad\text{for every }k=0,\cdots,n-1.\end{gathered}

In particular, if also
{\mathfrak{y}}^{N}
belongs to
{\Gamma_{1}}
then
P\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}^{0}\to{\mathfrak{y}}^{N}|V_{\Gamma},\mathcal{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma})
. More
generally,
if the initial and final points of a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk

P
belong to
V_{\Gamma}
then all the points of
P
belong to
V_{\Gamma}
.

Definition 5.4

(
\mathsf{H}
-connectedness)
.

We say that a set
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
is
\mathsf{H}
-connected if
the graph
(V_{\Gamma},\mathcal{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma})

is connected or,
equivalently, if every pair of points of
\Gamma_{1}
is

\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-connected.

Let us now define the
“oriented” cost function

\check{\mathsf{H}}:\mathsf{Z}\times\mathsf{Z}\to\bar{}\mathbb{R}
starting from a cost function
\mathsf{H}
as in (
5.1
):

\check{\mathsf{H}}(\mathfrak{y}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}^{\prime\prime}):=\begin{cases}-\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}^{\prime\prime})&\text{if }\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}],\ \mathfrak{y}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}],\\
+\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{y}^{\prime})&\text{if }\mathfrak{y}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}],\ \mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}],\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The cost
\Theta(P)
of a walk
P=(\mathfrak{y}^{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{y}^{N})
in
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{A}_{0,\Gamma})
with
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
is defined by

\Theta(P):=\sum_{h=1}^{N}\check{\mathsf{H}}(\mathfrak{y}^{h-1},\mathfrak{y}^{h});

notice that
\Theta
is well defined and takes values in

\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
since
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
, so that
the negative contributions of the arcs to
\Theta(P)
are always
finite. It is also easy to check that

(5.4)

\text{$\Theta(P)$ is finite if and only if $P$ is a $\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}$-walk}.

In the case of (
5.3
) we can equivalently write

\Theta(P)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\Big(\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{k+1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{k})-\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{k},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{k})\Big).

Clearly,
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone according to Definition
5.1
if and only if

(5.5)

\text{for every cycle $P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{y}\to\mathfrak{y})$ in $(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{A}_{0,\Gamma})$
we have }\quad\Theta(P)\geq 0.

Recalling (
5.4
) it is immediate to see that it is sufficient
to check
condition (
5.5
) on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-cycles in
V_{\Gamma}
.
We are going to use the following notation: if
P=({\mathfrak{y}}^{0},\cdots,{\mathfrak{y}}^{N})
and

A=({\mathfrak{y}}^{k},{\mathfrak{y}}^{k+1})
is an internal arc for some
0<k<N-1
, we can “remove” the arc

A
from
P
obtaining a new walk
P^{-k}

by setting
P^{-k}:=({\mathfrak{y}}^{0},\cdots,{\mathfrak{y}}^{k-1},{\mathfrak{y}}^{k+1},{\mathfrak{y}}^{N})
.

Lemma 5.5

(The effect of removing the vertex
({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})
)
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be satisfying the standard assumption
(
5.1
)
and let
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
be a radial cone.

If
P
is a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk
and
P_{\mathfrak{o}}
is the walk obtained removing from
P
all the internal arcs of the form
({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})
, then

(5.6)

P_{\mathfrak{o}}\text{ is a $\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}$-walk and}\quad\Theta(P_{\mathfrak{o}})\leq\Theta(P).

Proof.

Let
P=({\mathfrak{y}}^{0},\cdots,{\mathfrak{y}}^{N})
be a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk.
The proof follows by a simple induction argument if we show that

\Theta
decreases if we remove an internal arc of the form

({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})
. We can thus assume that

{\mathfrak{y}}^{k}={\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}^{k+1}={\mathfrak{o}}_{2}
for some
0<k<N

and we set

P^{\prime}:=P^{-k}
.
We notice that
{\mathfrak{y}}^{k-1}=[x^{k-1},r^{k-1}]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
and

{\mathfrak{y}}^{k+2}=[x^{k+2},r^{k+2}]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]
so that

\displaystyle\Theta(P)-\Theta(P^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\check{\mathsf{H}}({\mathfrak{y}}^{k-1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{1})+\check{\mathsf{H}}({\mathfrak{o}}_{2},{\mathfrak{y}}^{k+2})-\check{\mathsf{H}}({\mathfrak{y}}^{k-1},{\mathfrak{y}}^{k+2})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}^{k-1})+\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{y}}^{k+2},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})-\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{y}}^{k+2},{\mathfrak{y}}^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}_{x^{k+2},x^{k-1}}(0,r^{k-1})+\mathsf{H}_{x^{k+2},x^{k-1}}(r^{k+2},0)-\mathsf{H}_{x^{k+2},x^{k-1}}(r^{k+2},r^{k-1})\geq 0

thanks to the subadditivity of
\mathsf{H}_{x^{k+2},x^{k-1}}
.
The same argument shows that
({\mathfrak{y}}^{k-1},{\mathfrak{y}}^{k+2})\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}}
so that
P^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk.

∎

Corollary 5.6
.

Under the same assumptions of Lemma
5.5
,
if
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-connected then
also
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}:=\Gamma\setminus\{({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\}

is
\mathsf{H}
-connected and if
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}
is

\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone then also
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically
monotone (i.e. the arc
(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})
is irrelevant for
\mathsf{H}
-connectedness
and
\mathsf{H}
cyclical monotonicity).

Proof.

Let
\Gamma_{{\mathfrak{o}},i}:=\pi^{i}(\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}})

and let
{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{{\mathfrak{o}},1}.

If both are different from

{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}
, since
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-connected and
{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{1}
, we can find a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk
P\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}\to{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime})
and, by the previous Lemma
5.5
we can remove
from
P
all the internal arcs of the form
({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})

obtaining
a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}}
-walk
P_{\mathfrak{o}}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}\to{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime})
.

If
{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{o}}_{1}\neq{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime}
, since
{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}\in\pi^{1}(\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}})
, we
can find

{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}\}
such that

({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime})\in\Gamma

and, by the same argument above, a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}}
-walk
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime}\to{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime})
. It
follows that
P:=({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime})+P^{\prime}
is a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}}
-walk connecting
{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}
to
{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime}
.

If
{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}\neq{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}={\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime}
,
we can find
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}\}

such that

({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime\prime})\in\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
, so that
in particular
\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime\prime})<\infty
and
({\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime\prime},{\mathfrak{o}}_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}}
. By the same argument above, we can find
P^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}\to{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime\prime})
which is a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}}
-walk
so that
P:=P^{\prime\prime}+({\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime\prime},{\mathfrak{o}}_{1})\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}\to{\mathfrak{o}}_{1})

is a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}}
-walk as well.

If
{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime}={\mathfrak{o}}_{1}
, the two arguments above show that we can find
{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}_{2},{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime}_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}\}
such that
({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime})\in\Gamma
and
({\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime\prime},{\mathfrak{o}}_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}}
. Since both
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma_{2}
are different from
{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}
, we can apply again Lemma
5.5
and obtain a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}}
-walk
P^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime}\to{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime\prime})
. We can thus define
P:=({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime})+P^{\prime\prime\prime}+({\mathfrak{y}}_{2}^{\prime\prime},{\mathfrak{o}}_{1})\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime}\to{\mathfrak{y}}^{\prime\prime})
which is a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}}
-walk.

Let us now suppose that
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}
is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone
and let
P
be a
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-cycle in
V_{\Gamma}
.
It is not restrictive to suppose that
P
contains at least an
element

{\mathfrak{y}}\in V_{\Gamma}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}\}
(otherwise

\Theta(P)=0
). By a cyclic permutation of the arcs of
P
we
can assume that the initial and final point of
P
is
{\mathfrak{y}}
.
By Lemma
5.5
we can construct a cycle
P_{\mathfrak{o}}
in

(V_{\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}})
obtained by removing all
the arcs
({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})
from
P
with the same initial and final
point. Since
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}
is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone, we
have
\Theta(P_{\mathfrak{o}})\geq 0
. The inequality (
5.6
) shows that

\Theta(P)\geq 0
as well.
∎

The next Proposition shows how
\mathsf{H}
-cyclical monotonicity reflects on walks that are not precisely a cycle (cf. (
5.5
)) but are of the form
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{y}\to\lambda\mathfrak{y})
for some
\lambda>0
.

Given
(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})=([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
, we denote by
\partial\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})
the subdifferential of
\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}

at the point
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
, defined as

\partial\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}):=\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ :|\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}(s_{1},s_{2})-\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})\geq a(s_{1}-r_{1})+b(s_{2}-r_{2})\\
\text{ for every }(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\end{array}\right\}.

Notice that

\partial\mathsf{H}(\lambda\mathfrak{y}_{1},\lambda\mathfrak{y}_{2})=\partial\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})
for every
\lambda>0
.
As usual, the
proper domain of the subdifferential is denoted by

{\mathrm{D}}(\partial\mathsf{H}):=\left\{(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\mathfrak{C}[X_{1},X_{2}]\mid\partial\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\neq\emptyset\right\}
.
It is well known that

{\mathrm{D}}(\partial\mathsf{H})

contains
the
radial interior

\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right):=\Big\{(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\mathfrak{C}[X_{1},X_{2}]:(r_{1},r_{2})\in\operatorname{int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}})\right)\Big\},

where the interior of
{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}})

refers to the usual topology of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
;
in particular, if
(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)
, then
r_{1}>0,r_{2}>0
.

For every
(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)
we can consider the quantity

(5.7)

\mathsf{a}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}):=\sup\Big\{|a|:(a,b)\in\partial\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\Big\};

notice that

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}(\lambda\mathfrak{y}_{1},\lambda\mathfrak{y}_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{a}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})

\displaystyle\quad\text{for every }\lambda>0,

\displaystyle-\mathsf{a}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})|b|\leq ab

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{a}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})|b|

\displaystyle\quad\text{for every }(a,b)\in\partial\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}).

If
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}={\mathfrak{o}}_{2}
and
({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\in{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
we also set

\mathsf{a}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}):=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})=r_{1}^{-1}\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\quad\text{where }{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}=[x_{1},r_{1}].

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be as in
(
5.1
), let
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})

be
a

\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone radial cone, and
let
({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})=([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}=\Gamma\setminus\{({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\}
,
such that

({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})\in\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)

(and therefore
r_{i}>0
)
or
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}={\mathfrak{o}}_{2}
(and therefore
r_{1}>0
). Let
\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime}=[x_{1},r_{1}^{\prime}]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]
with
r_{1}^{\prime}>0
; then:

(1)

for every
\varepsilon>0

there exists a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}}
-walk
P_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{y}_{1})

with

(5.8)

\Theta(P_{\varepsilon})\leq\mathsf{a}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})|r_{1}^{\prime}-r_{1}|+\varepsilon;

(2)

if
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}\to\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime})
is any
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk,
then

(5.9)

\Theta(P)\geq-\mathsf{a}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})|r_{1}^{\prime}-r_{1}|.

Proof.

Notice that
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}\}
;
if
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}\neq{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}
we set

\bar{\mathsf{H}}:=\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}
,
q:=\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}>0
;

Claim (1).
The case
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}={\mathfrak{o}}_{2}
is simple: since
({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\in\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})

we have
\mathsf{a}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})=r_{1}^{-1}\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})<\infty
;
since
\Gamma
is a radial cone and
({\mathfrak{y}}_{1}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})=r_{1}^{\prime}/r_{1}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})
we have
({\mathfrak{y}}_{1}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\in\Gamma
with

\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})=r_{1}^{\prime}\mathsf{a}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})
.
The walk
P=({\mathfrak{y}}_{1}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2},{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})
belongs
to
(V_{\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}},\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}})

and

\Theta(P)=\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})-\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})=(r_{1}-r_{1}^{\prime})\mathsf{a}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})\leq\mathsf{a}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})|r_{1}-r_{1}^{\prime}|.

Let us now consider the case

{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}\neq{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define

\vartheta_{n}:=\left(\frac{r_{1}}{r_{1}^{\prime}}\right)^{1/n}

and we consider the points

\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{k}:=\left[x_{1},r_{1}^{\prime}\left({\vartheta_{n}}\right)^{k}\right],\quad\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{k}:=\left[x_{2},r_{1}^{\prime}q\left({\vartheta_{n}}\right)^{k}\right],\quad k=0,\dots,n-1,\quad\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{n}:=\left[x_{1},r_{1}\right]=\mathfrak{y}_{1},

inducing a walk
P_{n}
connecting
\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime}
to
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
according to (
5.3
),
since

\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{0}=[x_{1},r_{1}^{\prime}]=\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\quad(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{k},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{k})=(\rho_{n,k}[x_{1},r_{1}],\rho_{n,k}[x_{2},r_{2}])\in{\Gamma},\quad k=0,\dots,n-1,

where
\rho_{n,k}=\frac{r_{1}^{\prime}}{r_{1}}\left({\vartheta_{n}}\right)^{k}
. We have

\displaystyle\Theta(P_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\left(\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{k+1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{k})-\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{k},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{k})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\left(\bar{\mathsf{H}}\left(r_{1}^{\prime}\left({\vartheta_{n}}\right)^{k+1},r_{1}^{\prime}q\left({\vartheta_{n}}\right)^{k}\right)-\bar{\mathsf{H}}\left(r_{1}^{\prime}\left({\vartheta_{n}}\right)^{k},r_{1}^{\prime}q\left({\vartheta_{n}}\right)^{k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{1}^{\prime}\left({\vartheta_{n}}\right)^{k}\left(\bar{\mathsf{H}}({\vartheta_{n}},q)-\bar{\mathsf{H}}\left(1,q\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}r_{1}^{\prime}\left({\vartheta_{n}}\right)^{k}\left(1-{\vartheta_{n}}\right)a_{n}=r_{1}^{\prime}a_{n}\left(1-\left({\vartheta_{n}}\right)^{n}\right)=a_{n}(r_{1}^{\prime}-r_{1})

where

(a_{n},b_{n})\in\partial\bar{H}\left({\theta_{n}},q\right)\neq\emptyset

for
n
sufficiently large, since
\left({\theta_{n}},q\right)\to(1,q)

as
n\to+\infty
and

\partial\bar{\mathsf{H}}(1,q)=\partial\bar{\mathsf{H}}(r_{1},r_{2})

with
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\operatorname{int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\partial\bar{\mathsf{H}})\right)=\operatorname{int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\bar{\mathsf{H}})\right)
. This proves that
P_{n}
is a walk in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}}
and, passing to the
\limsup
as
n\to\infty
we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}a_{n}(r_{1}^{\prime}-r_{1})\leq\mathsf{a}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})|r_{1}^{\prime}-r_{1}|

which yields (
5.8
).

Claim (2).
If
P\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}^{\prime})

is any
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk
connecting

\mathfrak{y}_{1}
to
\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime}
, we can consider the cycle
\tilde{P}_{\varepsilon}=P+P_{\varepsilon}

starting and ending in
\mathfrak{y}_{1}

obtained by joining
P
with the walk
P_{\varepsilon}
given by the previous
claim. (
5.5
) and the
\mathsf{H}
-cyclycal monotonicity of
\Gamma
yield

0\leq\Theta(\tilde{P}_{\varepsilon})=\Theta(P)+\Theta(P_{\varepsilon})

which immediately gives (
5.9
).
∎

We can now give sufficient conditions for the
\mathsf{H}
-connectedness of
a set
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
, with
\mathsf{H}:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\to[0,+\infty]
as in (
5.1
).
We set

\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}):=\inf\Big\{\mathsf{H}(\lambda_{1}\mathfrak{y}_{1},\lambda_{2}\mathfrak{y}_{2}):\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>0\Big\},\quad(\mathfrak{y}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}].

Notice that
we have

(5.10)

\text{if }\mathfrak{y}_{i}=[x_{i},r_{i}]\neq\mathfrak{o}_{i}\text{ then }\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<\infty\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\exists\,s_{i}>0:\mathsf{H}([x_{1},s_{1}],[x_{2},s_{2}])<\infty

whereas

(5.11)

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})<\infty\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})<\infty,\\
\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<\infty\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<\infty.\end{gathered}

We can also easily see that

\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<\infty
if and only
if it is possibile to connect
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}
)
to a multiple of
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}
(resp. of
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
) with finite
\mathsf{H}
-cost:

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<\infty

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\exists\,\lambda_{2}>0:\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\lambda_{2}{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})<\infty

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\exists\,\lambda_{1}>0:\mathsf{H}(\lambda_{1}{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})<\infty.

Theorem 5.8

(Sufficient conditions for

\mathsf{H}
-connectedness)
.

If
\mathsf{H}
is as in (
5.1
) and
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
is a
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone radial cone satisfying at least
one of the following conditions:

(1)

\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}
-connected
and

\Gamma\setminus\Big(\{\mathfrak{o}_{1}\}\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\cup\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\{\mathfrak{o}_{2}\}\Big)\subset\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)
,

(2)

\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}
is finite on
{\Gamma_{1}}\times{\Gamma_{2}}

(recall (
5.2
))
and

(5.12)

\Gamma\cap\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)\neq\emptyset,

(3)

\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}
is finite on
{\Gamma_{1}}\times{\Gamma_{2}}
and

(5.13)

\text{there exist points $\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{i}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{i}\}$ such that
$(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\in\Gamma$ and $({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})\in\Gamma$},

then

\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-connected.

Proof.

We divide the proof in claims.

Claim (1). It is sufficient to prove that
if a pair of points
(\mathfrak{y}_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1})\in{\Gamma_{2}}\times{\Gamma_{1}}

satisfies
\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<+\infty

then we can find a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
walk
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{y}_{2}\to\mathfrak{y}_{1})
.
If
\mathfrak{y}_{1}=\mathfrak{o}_{1}
or
\mathfrak{y}_{2}=\mathfrak{o}_{2}
we have
\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<+\infty
thanks to (
5.11
),
so that the arc
(\mathfrak{y}_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1})
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}}
.

We can thus suppose that
\mathfrak{y}_{i}=[x_{i},r_{i}]
with
r_{i}>0
.
Recalling (
5.10
) and using the

1
-homogeneity of
\mathsf{H}
we can find
r_{1}^{\prime}>0
such
that, setting
\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime}=[x_{1},r_{1}^{\prime}]
, we get
\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<+\infty
. On the other hand, since
\mathfrak{y}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}
we can also find
\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{\prime}
such that

(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{\prime})\in\Gamma
.

If
\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{o}_{2}
then
(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{o}_{2})\in\Gamma
as well, since

\Gamma
is a radial cone, and
\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})<+\infty
since

\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
. We conclude that

P=(\mathfrak{y}_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{o}_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1})
is an admissible walk in

(V_{\Gamma},\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma})
.

If
\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{\prime}\neq\mathfrak{o}_{2}
then
(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{\prime})\in\Gamma\cap\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)
and we can apply
the first claim of Proposition
5.7
to find a
walk

P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{y}_{1})

in

(V_{\Gamma},\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma})
. Joining
(\mathfrak{y}_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime})

with
P
we obtain a connection from
\mathfrak{y}_{2}
to
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
.

Claim (2).
Let us pick
(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})\in\Gamma\cap\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)
,
\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{i}=[\bar{x}_{i},\bar{r}_{i}]
with
\bar{r}_{i}>0
and
\bar{q}:=\bar{r}_{2}/\bar{r}_{1}
. It is sufficient to show that for every
\mathfrak{y}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}
we can find a walk
P^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to\mathfrak{y}_{1})
and a walk
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}\to\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})
in
(V_{\Gamma},\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma})
.

Since
\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})<\infty
,
we can find
\mathfrak{y}_{2}=[\bar{x}_{2},r_{2}]
with
r_{2}>0

such that
\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<+\infty
, so that
(\mathfrak{y}_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
.
Since
\Gamma
is a radial cone, setting
\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime}:=[\bar{x}_{1},r_{2}/\bar{q}]
we have

(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\Gamma
; moreover
(r_{2}/\bar{q},r_{2})=r_{2}/\bar{r}_{2}(\bar{r}_{1},\bar{r}_{2})\in\operatorname{int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H}_{\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2}})\right)
so
that
(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)
.
By Claim (1) of Proposition

5.7

we can eventually find a walk
\tilde{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime})

in
(V_{\Gamma},\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma})
. Thus joining
\tilde{P}
with
(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1})
we obtain a walk
P^{\prime\prime}
connecting
\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
to
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
.

In order to find the second walk connecting
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
to
\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
,
we first select
\mathfrak{y}_{2}=[x_{2},r_{2}]
so that
(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\Gamma
(using
the fact that
\mathfrak{y}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}
). Since
\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<\infty
we find
\lambda_{1}>0
such that

\mathsf{H}(\lambda_{1}\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<\infty
. By Proposition
5.7

we can eventually join
\lambda_{1}\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}

to
\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
by a walk
\tilde{P}
in

(V_{\Gamma},\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma})
.
The walk
P^{\prime}
obtained by joining
(\mathfrak{y}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2},\lambda_{1}\bar{\mathfrak{y}_{1}})

with
\tilde{P}
provides the requested connection from
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
to
\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
in
(V_{\Gamma},\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma})
.

Claim (3). The proof is quite similar to the previous claim. We show that, for every
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}
, we can connect
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
to
{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}
to
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
with a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk. Let
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}
be fixed.
Since
\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})<\infty
,
we can find
\mathfrak{y}_{2}=\lambda_{2}\bar{{\mathfrak{y}}}_{2}
,
\lambda_{2}>0
,
such that
\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<+\infty
, so that
(\mathfrak{y}_{2},\mathfrak{y}_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
. The
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk

({\mathfrak{o}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2},{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})

connects
{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}
to
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
.

In order to find the second walk connecting
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
to
{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}

we first select
\mathfrak{y}_{2}=[x_{2},r_{2}]
so that
(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\Gamma
(using
the fact that
\mathfrak{y}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}
). Since
\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<\infty
we find
\lambda>0
such that

\mathsf{H}(\lambda_{1}\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})<\infty
. Since
\Gamma
is a radial cone,

(\lambda\bar{{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2})\in\Gamma
so that
the walk
P^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2},\lambda\bar{{\mathfrak{y}}}_{1},{\mathfrak{o}}_{2},{\mathfrak{o}}_{1})
connects

{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
to
{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}
and belongs to
(V_{\Gamma},\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma})
.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

If
there is a common
vector

(r_{1},r_{2})\neq(0,0)

in the intersection
\bigcap\limits_{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2}}{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}})
of all the domains of the functions

\mathsf{H}_{x_{1},x_{2}}
,
then

\mathsf{H}_{\rm inf}
is finite in
(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}\})\times(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}\})
. Then, if
\Gamma\subset(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{1}\})\times(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{2}\})
and
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)
, condition (1) of Theorem

5.8
is satisfied and
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-connected.
If
\mathsf{H}
is finite
then it is immediate to check that any
\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
is
\mathsf{H}
-connected.

5.2.
Sufficient conditions for optimality

The next result provides
sufficient conditions in order to
guarantee the optimality of a
1
-homogeneous coupling concentrated on a

\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone radial cone
\Gamma
.
The main assumptions concern

\mathsf{H}
-connectedness of
\Gamma

(see the previous Theorem

5.8

for simple conditions guaranteeing this property),
the fact that
\Gamma
has nonempty intersection with the radial interior

\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)
of
{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})

(or, alternatively, that (
5.13
) holds)
and an
integrability condition (
5.16
)
which minimic the usual condition
stated in the framework of balanced OT.
To avoid trivial cases, we will
assume that the measures
\mu_{i}
have strictly positive mass.
We also mention that the result below provides the existence of a relaxed solution for the dual problem, i.e. a pair of optimal potentials that are only Borel measurable.

In order to treat potentials that may take infinite values, we use the notation

\zeta_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+_{o}\zeta_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}:=\lim_{n\to+\infty}(-n\vee\zeta_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}\wedge n)+(-n\vee\zeta_{1}(x_{1})r_{2}\wedge n)

for functions
\zeta_{i}:\mathsf{X}_{i}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
, with the convention that
\pm\infty\cdot 0=0
. In particular
\zeta_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+_{o}\zeta_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}=0
in case
\zeta_{1}(x_{1})=\pm\infty
,
\zeta_{2}(x_{2})=\mp\infty
and
r_{1},r_{2}>0
.

Theorem 5.10

(Sufficiency of
\mathsf{H}
-cyclical monotonicity)
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be as in (
5.1
),
let
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i})
with
\mu_{i}(\mathsf{X}_{i})>0
for
i=1,2
, and let
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
be an admissible
1
-homogeneous coupling
concentrated on a
\sigma
-compact
radial cone
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})

such that

\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone and
\mathsf{H}
-connected.
We set

{\Gamma_{i}}:=\pi^{i}(\Gamma),\quad S_{i}:=\mathsf{x}({\Gamma_{i}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{i}\}).

(1)

If
\Gamma
satisfies one of the two conditions
(
5.12
) or (
5.13
)
then the measures
\mu_{i}
are concentrated on the sets
S_{i}

(in particular
S_{i}\neq\emptyset
) and
there exist Borel functions
\varphi_{i}:\mathsf{X}_{i}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
which are real valued on
S_{i}
and such that

(5.14)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+_{o}\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{for every }x_{i}\in\mathsf{X}_{i},\ r_{i}\geq 0,

(5.15)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{if
}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\Gamma.

(2)

If moreover there exist nonnegative Borel functions
\varrho_{i}\in L^{1}_{+}(\mathsf{X}_{i},\mu_{i})
such that

(5.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mu_{1}\left(\left\{x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}\mid\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\varrho_{2}(x_{2})],[x_{2},1])\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x_{2})<+\infty\right\}\right)>0,\\
&\mu_{2}\left(\left\{x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}\mid\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},\varrho_{1}(x_{1})])\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{1})<+\infty\right\}\right)>0,\end{split}

then
\bm{\alpha}
is optimal,
\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}<+\infty
, the functions
\varphi_{i}
belong to

\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathsf{X}_{i},\mu_{i})
and provide a relaxed solution
for the dual problem (
3.31
) i.e.

\begin{gathered}\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+_{o}\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{for every }x_{i}\in\mathsf{X}_{i},\ r_{i}\geq 0,\\
\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).\end{gathered}

Proof.

We set

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}:=\Gamma\setminus\{(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})\},\quad\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{o}}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]:=\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\{(\mathfrak{o}_{1},\mathfrak{o}_{2})\}.

We first construct a candidate function
\Phi:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\to\mathbb{R}

inducing a potential
\zeta_{1}
via the identity

\Phi([x_{1},r_{1}])=\zeta_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}
.

Step 1 (Definition of
\Phi
in
{\Gamma_{1}}
)
. We pick a point
(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})=([\bar{x}_{1},\bar{r}_{1}],[\bar{x}_{2},\bar{r}_{2}])\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{o}}
such that

(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})\in\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)
(condition (
5.12
)) or
\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}={\mathfrak{o}}_{2}

(condition (
5.13
)).
We set (see (
5.7
))

\bar{\mathsf{a}}:={\mathsf{a}}(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}).

We define
\Phi:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]\to[-\infty,+\infty]
as

(5.17)

\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1}):=\inf\Big\{\Theta(P)+\bar{\mathsf{a}}|r_{1}-\lambda\bar{r}_{1}|:P\in\mathcal{P}(\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}),\lambda>0\Big\},\quad\mathfrak{y}_{1}=[x_{1},r_{1}]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}].

Since
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-connected and
\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\in\Gamma_{1}
,
for every
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}
we can find

P\in\mathcal{P}(\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})
with finite cost
\Theta(P)

so that

\Phi({\mathfrak{y}}_{1})<+\infty\quad\text{for every }{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}.

In particular, choosing
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}:=[\bar{x}_{1},r]=\alpha\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
,
r>0
,
with
\alpha:=r/\bar{r}_{1}
,
\lambda=\alpha
,
and
P=(\alpha\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\alpha\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2},\alpha\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})
we have
\Theta(P)=0
and we immediately get

(5.18)

\Phi([\bar{x}_{1},r])\leq 0\quad\text{for every }r>0.

By Proposition
5.7
,
every walk
P\in\mathcal{P}(\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})
,
\lambda>0

satisfies

\Theta(P)\geq-\mathsf{a}(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})|\lambda\bar{r}_{1}-\bar{r}_{1}|\quad\text{ for every }\lambda>0,

so that (
5.17
) yields
\Phi(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})\geq 0
;
combining with (
5.18
) we obtain

\Phi(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})=0.

Since for every
\mathfrak{y}_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]
,
\lambda>0
,
P=(\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}^{1},\cdots,{\mathfrak{y}}^{N-1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})\in\mathcal{P}(\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})

and every
\alpha>0
we have

P_{\alpha}:=(\alpha\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\alpha{\mathfrak{y}}^{1},\cdots,\alpha{\mathfrak{y}}^{N-1},\alpha{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})\in\mathcal{P}(\alpha\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\alpha{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})

with

\Theta(P_{\alpha})+\bar{\mathsf{a}}|\alpha r_{1}-\alpha\lambda\bar{r}_{1}|=\alpha\Big(\Theta(P)+\bar{\mathsf{a}}|r_{1}-\lambda\bar{r}_{1}|\Big)

(since
\Gamma
is a radial cone and
\mathsf{H}
is positively
1
-homogeneous)
we get that

\Phi(\alpha\mathfrak{y}_{1})=\alpha\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1})\quad\text{ for
every }\mathfrak{y}_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}],\ \alpha>0.

If now
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}
and
\lambda>0
,
we use the
\mathsf{H}
-connectedness of
\Gamma
to find a
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H},\Gamma}
-walk

\bar{P}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})
.
If
P\in\mathcal{P}(\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})
,
P+\bar{P}
is a walk
in
\mathcal{P}(\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})

so that (
5.9
) and
the identity
{\mathsf{a}}(\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})={\mathsf{a}}(\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})=\bar{\mathsf{a}}

yield

-\bar{\mathsf{a}}|\lambda-1|\bar{r}_{1}\leq\Theta(P+\bar{P})=\Theta(P)+\Theta(\bar{P})

so that

\Phi({\mathfrak{y}}_{1})\geq-\Theta(\bar{P})-{\mathsf{a}}|r_{1}-\bar{r}_{1}|>-\infty.

Arguing as in
[
AGS08
, Step 1 of Therem 6.14]
and using the fact that
\Gamma
is a
\sigma
-compact
set, we can see that

\Phi
is a Borel function.

Let us now consider
\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime}\in{\Gamma_{1}}
,

\mathfrak{y}_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{2}]
such that

(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in{\Gamma}
,
and any walk
P\in\mathcal{P}(\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\to{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})
;
if we consider the walk
P^{\prime}=P+({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2},{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}^{\prime})
,

P^{\prime}
is an admissible walk joining
\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}
to
{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}^{\prime}

so that

\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}_{2})-\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})+\Theta(P)+\bar{\mathsf{a}}|r_{1}-\lambda\bar{r}_{1}|.

Passing then to the infimum among all the walks
P\in\mathcal{P}(\lambda\bar{\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{1})
,
\lambda>0
,
we deduce that

\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime})\leq\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1})+\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}_{2})-\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\quad\text{for every }\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime}\in{\Gamma_{1}},\ (\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\Gamma.

Restricting
\Phi
to
{\Gamma_{1}}
(notice that
\Gamma_{i}
is Borel since
\Gamma
is
\sigma
-compact) we have proven that there exists a Borel
function
\Phi:{\Gamma_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(5.19)

\displaystyle\Phi([\bar{x}_{1},r_{1}])

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\quad\text{ for every }r_{1}>0,

(5.20)

\displaystyle\Phi(\lambda\mathfrak{y}_{1})

\displaystyle=\lambda\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1})\quad

\displaystyle\quad\text{ for every }\mathfrak{y}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}},\ \lambda>0,

(5.21)

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1})+\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{y}_{2})-\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\quad

\displaystyle\quad\text{ for every }\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{\prime}\in{\Gamma_{1}},\ (\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in{\Gamma}.

Step 2 (Definition of
\Psi
)
. We define
\Psi:{\Gamma_{2}}\to[-\infty,+\infty]
as

\Psi(\mathfrak{y}_{2}):=\inf_{\mathfrak{y}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}}\{\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})-\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1})\},\quad\mathfrak{y}_{2}\in\Gamma_{2}.

It is clear from the definition that

\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1})+\Psi(\mathfrak{y}_{2})\leq\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\quad\text{ for every }(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in{\Gamma_{1}}\times{\Gamma_{2}}.

Since
\Phi
is real valued on
{\Gamma_{1}}
and for every
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}\in{\Gamma_{2}}
there
exists

{\mathfrak{y}}_{1}\in{\Gamma_{1}}
such that
({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})\in\Gamma
so that

\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})<\infty
,
we immediately get
\Psi({\mathfrak{y}}_{2})<\infty
for every
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}\in{\Gamma_{2}}
.
By the definition of
\Psi
, the fact that
\Gamma
is a radial cone,
the radial
1
-homogeneity of
\mathsf{H}
and of
\Phi
given by
(
5.20
),
it also easily follows that

\Psi(\lambda\mathfrak{y}_{2})=\lambda\Psi(\mathfrak{y}_{2})\quad\text{ for
every }\mathfrak{y}_{2}\in{\Gamma_{2}},\ \lambda>0.

The inequality in (
5.21
) immediately yields

\Psi(\mathfrak{y}_{2})+\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1})=\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\quad\text{ for every }(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in{\Gamma}

and also gives that
\Psi(\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}
for every
\mathfrak{y}_{2}\in{\Gamma_{2}}
.
The Borel measurability of
\Psi
can be checked as in
[
AGS08
, Step 2 of
Theorem 6.14]
.

Summarizing the second step, we have proven that there exists a Borel function
\Psi:{\Gamma_{2}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\Phi({\mathfrak{y}}_{1})+\Psi({\mathfrak{y}}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{H}({\mathfrak{y}}_{1},{\mathfrak{y}}_{2})\quad

\displaystyle\quad\text{ on }{\Gamma_{1}}\times{\Gamma_{2}},

(5.23)

\displaystyle\Psi(\lambda\mathfrak{y}_{2})

\displaystyle=\lambda\Psi(\mathfrak{y}_{2})\quad

\displaystyle\quad\text{ for every }\mathfrak{y}_{2}\in{\Gamma_{2}},\ \lambda>0,

(5.24)

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathfrak{y}_{1})+\Psi(\mathfrak{y}_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\quad

\displaystyle\quad\text{ for every }(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in{\Gamma}.

Step 3 (Definition of
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
)
. First of all notice that
S_{i}
is Borel since
\Gamma
is
\sigma
-compact. The following chain of inequalities shows that
\mu_{i}
is concentrated on
S_{i}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{i}\left(\mathsf{X}_{i}\setminus S_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{1}(\bm{\alpha})\left(\mathsf{X}_{i}\setminus\mathsf{x}_{i}\left(\Gamma\setminus\{\mathfrak{y}_{i}=\mathfrak{o}_{i}\}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{r}_{i}\bm{\alpha})\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}\left(\mathsf{X}_{i}\setminus\mathsf{x}_{i}\left(\Gamma\setminus\{\mathfrak{y}_{i}=\mathfrak{o}_{i}\}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{r}_{i}\bm{\alpha})\left(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}\left(\Gamma\setminus\{\mathfrak{y}_{i}=\mathfrak{o}_{i}\}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq(\mathsf{r}_{i}\bm{\alpha})\left(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\left(\Gamma\setminus\{\mathfrak{y}_{i}=\mathfrak{o}_{i}\}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq(\mathsf{r}_{i}\bm{\alpha})\left(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\Gamma\right)+(\mathsf{r}_{i}\bm{\alpha})\left(\Gamma\cap\{\mathfrak{y}_{i}=\mathfrak{o}_{i}\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\Gamma}\mathsf{r}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}+\int_{\{\mathsf{r}_{i}=0\}}\mathsf{r}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0.

Since we have assumed that the two measures
\mu_{i}
are positive, this also shows that
S_{i}\neq\emptyset
,
i=1,2
.
Let us
define
\zeta_{i}:S_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\zeta_{1}(x_{1}):=\Phi([x_{1},1]),\quad\zeta_{2}(x_{2}):=\Psi([x_{2},1]),\quad x_{i}\in S_{i}=\mathsf{x}({\Gamma_{i}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{o}}_{i}\}).

Notice that
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}
are Borel functions.
We claim that

(5.25)

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\zeta_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{ for
every }x_{i}\in S_{i},r_{i}\geq 0,

(5.26)

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\zeta_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{ if }([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in{\Gamma}.

Notice that the product
\zeta(x_{i})r_{i}
in (
5.26
) has to be intended equal to
0
in case
x_{i}\notin S_{i}
and
r_{i}=0
.
We distinguish four cases:

(i)

if
r_{1}=r_{2}=0
, both sides in (
5.25
) and (
5.26
) are equal to
0
;

(ii)

if
r_{1},r_{2}\neq 0
, then
\Phi([x_{1},r_{1}])=r_{1}\zeta_{1}(x_{1})
and
\Psi([x_{2},r_{2}])=r_{2}\zeta_{2}(x_{2})
by (
5.20
)
and (
5.23
),
so that (
5.25
) corresponds to
(
5.22
) and
(
5.26
)
corresponds to
(
5.24
);

(iii)

if
r_{1}>0=r_{2}
, then
[x_{2},r_{2}]=\mathfrak{o}_{2}
and

\zeta_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}=0
:
for (
5.25
) we can just pass to the limit as

r_{2}\downarrow 0

in the same inequality, using the fact that

r_{2}\mapsto\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])
is continuous at
0
.
Regarding (
5.26
), observe that saying that
([x_{1},r_{1}],\mathfrak{o}_{2})\in\Gamma
gives in particular that
\mathfrak{o}_{2}\in\Gamma_{2}
so that (
5.23
) forces
\Psi(\mathfrak{o}_{2})=0
. Then (
5.26
), is exactly (
5.24
) with
{\mathfrak{y}}_{2}={\mathfrak{o}}_{2}
;

(iv)

the case
r_{1}=0<r_{2}
is completely analogous to the previous
one also using (
5.19
).

Now we can use the
\mathsf{H}
-transform (compare also with Definition
4.5
) to extend the potentials
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
to the whole spaces
\mathsf{X}_{i}
while keeping intact the relations in (
5.25
) and (
5.26
): we define
\varphi_{i}:\mathsf{X}_{i}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
as

(5.27)

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(x_{2})

\displaystyle:=\inf_{x_{1}\in S_{1},r_{1}\geq 0}\left\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},1])-\zeta_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}\right\},\quad x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2},

(5.28)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})

\displaystyle:=\inf_{x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2},r_{2}\geq 0}\left\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}])-\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}\right\},\quad x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1},

with the convention that
\pm\infty\cdot 0=0
and
\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}])-\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}=+\infty
if
\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}])=+\infty
,
\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}=+\infty
.
It is not difficult to check that
\varphi_{i}
are Borel function,
\varphi_{i}(x_{i})\in\mathbb{R}
if
x_{i}\in S_{i}
and

(5.29)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+_{o}\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{for every }x_{i}\in\mathsf{X}_{i},\ r_{i}\geq 0,

(5.30)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{if
}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\Gamma.

Notice that on
\Gamma
there is no need to use
+_{o}
since either
x_{i}\in S_{i}
(hence
\varphi(x_{i})\in\mathbb{R}
) or
r_{i}=0
(hence
\varphi(x_{i})r_{i}=0
).

Step 4 (Conclusion)
. Since
\mu_{1}
is concentrated on
S_{1}
, using (
5.16
), we can find some
x_{1}\in S_{1}

such that
\int_{S_{2}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\varrho_{2}(x_{2})x],[x_{2},1])\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x_{2})<+\infty
;
by (
5.29
) we get that

\varphi_{1}(x_{1})\varrho_{2}(x_{2})+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\varrho_{2}(x_{2})],[x_{2},1])\quad\text{ for every }x_{2}\in S_{2}

so that

\varphi_{2}^{+}(x_{2})\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},\varrho_{2}(x_{2})],[x_{2},1])+\varphi_{1}(x_{1})^{-}\varrho_{2}(x_{2})\quad\text{ for every }x_{2}\in S_{2},

where we denoted by
u^{+}
and
u^{-}
the positive and negative part
respectively of a real number
u
.
This gives that
\varphi_{2}^{+}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathsf{X}_{2},\mu_{2})
. The argument for
\varphi_{1}
is the same. We can thus conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}(\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2},

showing that
\varphi_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathsf{X}_{i},\mu_{i})
. Moreover, if
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
, setting
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}:=\pi^{i}_{\sharp}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}
, we have

\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}]}(\varphi_{i}\mathsf{r})^{\pm}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\alpha}_{i}=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}]}\varphi_{i}^{\pm}\mathsf{r}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\alpha}_{i}=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{i}}\varphi_{i}^{\pm}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\Rightarrow\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{i}]}\varphi_{i}\mathsf{r}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\alpha}_{i}=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{i}}\varphi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}

so that
(\varphi_{i}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i})\mathsf{r}_{i}\in L^{1}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}],\tilde{\bm{\alpha}})
and thus
(\varphi_{1}\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}+(\varphi_{2}\circ\mathsf{x}_{2})\mathsf{r}_{2}\in L^{1}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}],\tilde{\bm{\alpha}})
. We deduce that the everywhere defined function

([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\mapsto\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+_{o}\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

is a representative of the
L^{1}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}],\tilde{\bm{\alpha}})
-equivalence class
(\varphi_{1}\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}+(\varphi_{2}\circ\mathsf{x}_{2})\mathsf{r}_{2}
(notice that the set where the sum would be undefined has null
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}
-measure). Hence, for every
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}(\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+_{o}\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

\displaystyle=\int((\varphi_{1}\circ\mathsf{x}_{1})\mathsf{r}_{1}+(\varphi_{2}\circ\mathsf{x}_{2})\mathsf{r}_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]}\varphi_{1}\mathsf{r}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\alpha}_{1}+\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1}]}\varphi_{2}\mathsf{r}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\alpha}_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}(\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]}\mathsf{H}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha},

showing both that
\bm{\alpha}
is optimal and that

\int_{\mathsf{X}_{1}}\varphi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}+\int_{\mathsf{X}_{2}}\varphi_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).\qed

Remark 5.11

(
\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)
and
S_{i}
)
.

In the same setting of Theorem
5.10
, since
\mu_{i}
is concentrated on
S_{i}
, we clearly have that
\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)\subset\overline{S_{i}}
. Moreover, we can assume that
S_{i}\subset\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)
since, up to defining

\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma\cap\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\mathsf{supp}\left(\bm{\alpha}\right)\right),

we see that
\bm{\alpha}
is still concentrated on
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
is also a
\sigma
-compact radial cone contained in
{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
which is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone and
\mathsf{H}
-connected. It is also easy to check that
\mathsf{x}(\tilde{\Gamma}_{i}\setminus\{\mathfrak{0}_{i}\})\subset\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)
. In this case we thus have
S_{i}\subset\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)=\overline{S_{i}}
.

Remark 5.12

(The case of
\mathsf{H}
finite)
.

In the same setting of Theorem
5.10
, in case the cost function
\mathsf{H}
does not attain the value
+\infty
, it is easy to check that the potentials
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
defined via the
\mathsf{H}
-transform of the pair (
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
as in (
5.27
) and (
5.28
) cannot attain the value
+\infty
so that the use of
+_{o}
is not needed in (
5.14
).

We conclude this section showing that, under suitable additional assumptions on
\mathsf{H}
, an optimal plan
\bm{\alpha}
must me induced by a transport-growth map as in Definition
3.19
in the sense that

\bm{\alpha}=([\text{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}},1],[\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g}])_{\sharp}\mu_{1},\quad\mu_{2}=(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}.

The proof is based on the classical approach to the existence of optimal transport maps (see e.g.
[
AGS08
, Theorem 6.2.4]
): one shows via the existence of optimal potentials as in Theorem
5.10
that the set on which
\bm{\alpha}
is concentrated is a graph. Condition (1) below is used to guarantee that the optimal potential
\varphi_{1}
is approximately differentiable at a.e. point, while condition (3) is the analogue of the classical twist condition (see e.g.
[
CD14
]
). On the other hand condition (2) (cp. with the condition in (
4.3
)) is used to prevent that
\mathfrak{o}_{1}
belongs to the first projection of the support of an optimal plan
\bm{\alpha}
: this corresponds to the fact that some of the mass of
\mu_{2}
does not come from
\mu_{1}
but it is created: clearly in this case there cannot be any transport-growth map connecting
\mu_{1}
to
\mu_{2}
.

Theorem 5.13

(Existence of an optimal transport-growth map)
.

Let
\mathsf{X}_{1}=\mathsf{X}_{2}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathsf{H}
be as in (
5.1
) and finite,
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mu_{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d})>0
for
i=1,2
. Assume that condition (
5.16
) is satisfied,
\mu_{1}\ll\mathcal{L}^{d}
and that
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})<+\infty
. Assume in addition that:

(1)

\mathsf{H}
is partially differentiable w.r.t.
x_{1},r_{1}
in

(\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\setminus\{\mathfrak{o}_{1}\})\times(\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\setminus\{\mathfrak{o}_{2}\})

with continuous partial derivatives;

(2)

for every
x_{2}\in\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{2}\right)
there exist
x_{1}\in\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{1}\right)
and
\varepsilon>0
such that
\mathrm{B}(x_{1},\varepsilon)\times\{x_{2}\}\subset A_{\mathsf{H}}
where

A_{\mathsf{H}}:=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:\partial_{r_{1}}\mathsf{H}(x,0;y,1)=\lim_{r_{1}\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathsf{H}([x,r_{1}],[y,1])-\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{o}_{1},[y,1])}{r_{1}}=-\infty\right\};

(3)

for every
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the map

[y,q]\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[y,q])\\
\partial_{r_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[y,q])\end{pmatrix}

is injective.

Then there exist Borel maps
(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,+\infty)
with
\mathsf{g}\in L^{1}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\mu_{1})
such that

\mu_{2}=(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})_{\star}\mu_{1}=\mathsf{T}_{\sharp}(\mathsf{g}\mu_{1}),\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[\mathsf{T}(x_{1}),\mathsf{g}(x_{1})])\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{1})=\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

given by the formula

(\mathsf{T},\mathsf{g})(x_{1})=(\partial_{x_{1},r_{1}}\mathsf{H})^{-1}(\tilde{\nabla}\varphi_{1}(x_{1}),\varphi_{1}(x_{1}))\quad\text{for $\mu_{1}$-a.e.\penalty\ }x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
(\partial_{x_{1},r_{1}}\mathsf{H})^{-1}
denotes the inverse of the map in item
3
and
\tilde{\nabla}
stands for the approximate differential.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})<+\infty
by assumption, Theorem
3.4
gives the existence of an optimal
1
-homogeneous coupling
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
concentrated on
\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\setminus\{(\mathfrak{0}_{1},\mathfrak{0}_{2})\}
. On the other hand, using Proposition
5.2
we get that
\bm{\alpha}
is concentrated on a
\sigma
-compact and
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone radial convex cone
\tilde{\Gamma}
. As in Remark
5.11
, we can define

\Gamma:=(\operatorname{\mathrm{hom}}\left(\mathsf{supp}\left(\bm{\alpha}\right)\right)\cap{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\cap\tilde{\Gamma})\setminus\{(\mathfrak{0}_{1},\mathfrak{0}_{2})\}

and we obtain that
\Gamma
is still a
\sigma
-compact radial cone which is
\mathsf{H}
-cyclically monotone, contained in
{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})
and such that
(\mathfrak{0}_{1},\mathfrak{0}_{2})\notin\Gamma
. Clearly
\bm{\alpha}
is concentrated on
\Gamma
and by Remark
5.11
we also have that
S_{i}:=\mathsf{x}(\Gamma_{i}\setminus\{\mathfrak{0}_{i}\})
is such that
S_{i}\subset\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{i}\right)=\overline{S_{i}}
. We want to apply Theorem
5.10
to deduce the existence of optimal potentials. To do so, we need to check that
\Gamma
is
\mathsf{H}
-connected and that at least one of the two conditions (
5.12
) or (
5.13
) is satisfied: since
\mathsf{H}
is everywhere finite and radially convex, then

\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)=\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{o}}[\mathbb{R}^{d}]\times\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{o}}[\mathbb{R}^{d}]=\{(\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2})\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathbb{R}^{d}]\times\mathfrak{C}[\mathbb{R}^{d}]:\mathfrak{y}_{i}\neq\mathfrak{0}_{i}\}.

This in particular gives that at least one of the conditions (
5.12
) or (
5.13
) must be satisfied by
\Gamma
(otherwise at least one between
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
must be the null measure, which is not allowed). Moreover,
\Gamma
is clearly
\mathsf{H}
-connected since
\mathsf{H}
is everywhere finite.

By Theorem
5.10
and Remark
5.12
, we can find Borel functions
\varphi_{i}:\mathsf{X}_{i}\to[-\infty,+\infty)
such that
\varphi_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu_{i})
,
\varphi_{i}(x_{i})\in\mathbb{R}
if
x_{i}\in S_{i}
and

(5.31)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{for every }x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ r_{i}\geq 0,

(5.32)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}+\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\quad\text{if
}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\Gamma.

We want to show that
\varphi_{1}
defined as above is approximately differential
\mu_{1}
-a.e. in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let us define, for every
R>0
, the functions

\varphi_{1}^{R}(x_{1}):=\inf_{(x_{2},r_{2})\in\mathrm{B}(0,R)\times[0,R]}\left\{\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}])-\varphi_{2}(x_{2})r_{2}\right\},\quad x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

By assumption
1
we deduce that
\varphi_{1}^{R}
is locally Lipschitz and therefore differentiable
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-a.e. for
R
sufficiently large. Moreover, by definition of
S_{1}
and since
\Gamma
is a radial cone, for every
x_{1}\in S_{1}
, we can find
[x_{2},r_{2}]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathbb{R}^{d}]
such that
([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\Gamma
so that (
5.32
) holds for
([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}])
. This, together with (
5.31
), implies that for
\mu_{1}
-a.e.
x_{1}\in S_{1}
the decreasing family of sets
\{\varphi<\varphi_{1}^{R}\}
has a
\mu_{1}
-negligible intersection, i.e.
\mu_{1}
-a.e.
x_{1}\in S_{1}
belongs to
\{\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{1}^{R}\}
for
R
large enough. It follows that for
\mu_{1}
-a.e.
x_{1}\in S_{1}
the following two conditions are satisfied:
x_{1}
is a point of density
1
of
\{\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{1}^{R}\}
for some
R
and
\varphi_{1}^{R}
is differentiable at
x_{1}
. We deduce that
\varphi_{1}
is approximately differentiable at
x_{1}
and
\tilde{\nabla}\varphi_{1}(x_{1})=\nabla\varphi_{1}^{R}(x_{1})
. Let us denote by
A_{1}\subset S_{1}
the full
\mu_{1}
-measure set where this happens.

Let now
([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\Gamma
with
x_{1}\in A_{1}
. Notice that the map

(x_{1}^{\prime},r_{1}^{\prime})\mapsto\mathsf{H}([x_{1}^{\prime},1],[x_{2},r_{2}])-\varphi_{1}(x_{1}^{\prime})r_{1}^{\prime}

attains its minimum at
(x_{1},r_{1})
. Let us show that it must be that
r_{1}>0
: if, by contradiction
r_{1}=0
, then we get

\mathsf{H}([x_{1}^{\prime},r_{1}^{\prime}],[x_{2},r_{2}])-\varphi_{1}(x_{1}^{\prime})r_{1}^{\prime}\geq\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{o}_{1},[x_{2},r_{2}])\text{ for every }(x_{1}^{\prime},r_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,+\infty).

Since
[x_{2},r_{2}]\neq\mathfrak{o}_{2}
then
x_{2}\in S_{2}
; by assumption
2
and since
\mathsf{supp}\left(\mu_{1}\right)
cannot contain isolated points, we can find some
x_{1}^{\prime}\in S_{1}
such that
(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})\in A_{\mathsf{H}}
. We can divide this expression by
r_{2}>0
so that

-\infty=\lim_{r_{1}^{\prime}\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathsf{H}([x_{1}^{\prime},r_{1}^{\prime}/r_{2}],[x_{2},1])-\mathsf{H}(\mathfrak{o}_{1},[x_{2},1])}{r_{1}^{\prime}}\geq\frac{1}{r_{2}}\varphi_{1}(x_{1}^{\prime}),

a contradiction with the fact that
\varphi_{1}(x_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}
.

By differentiation we get that

\displaystyle\tilde{\nabla}\varphi_{1}(x_{1})r_{1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]),

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=\partial_{r_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}]).

Using the radial
1
-homogeneity of
\mathsf{H}
and the radial
0
-homogeneity of its subdifferential in
r_{1}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\tilde{\nabla}\varphi_{1}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}/r_{1}]),

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x_{1})

\displaystyle=\partial_{r_{1}}\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}/r_{1}]).

By the invertibility assumption
3
, we deduce that

[x_{2},r_{2}/r_{1}]=(\partial_{x_{1},r_{1}}\mathsf{H})^{-1}(\tilde{\nabla}\varphi_{1}(x_{1}),\varphi_{1}(x_{1})).

Since the set
\Gamma\cap\mathsf{x}_{1}^{-1}(A_{1})
has full
\bm{\alpha}
measure (this follows by the fact that
0\notin\mathsf{r}_{1}(\Gamma)
), this concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

Theorem
5.13
could also be proven under a different set of hypotheses, namely dropping the assumption that
\mathsf{H}
is finite everywhere and substituting condition
2
by imposing that
\mathsf{H}
is finite on an open cone (cp. with (
B.1
)): there exists numbers
q_{i}\geq 0
such that, setting

U_{q_{1}q_{2}}:=\left\{([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}]\mid r_{2}>r_{1}q_{1},\,r_{1}>r_{2}q_{2}\right\},

we have
{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})=U_{q_{1}q_{2}}
. Clearly in this case the partial differentiability condition in
1
and the map in
3
should be restricted to
\{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathsf{H}([x_{1},1],[x_{2},r_{2}])<+\infty\}
and
\{[y,q]\in\mathfrak{C}[\mathbb{R}^{d}]:([x_{1},1],[y,q])\in{\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\}
, respectively. The proof stays unchanged and we only have to observe that in this case:

(1)

\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)={\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})=U_{q_{1}q_{2}}
so that
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{rad-int}\left({\mathrm{D}}(\mathsf{H})\right)
, hence condition (
5.12
) is satisfied. By Remark
5.9

\Gamma
is also
\mathsf{H}
-connected;

(2)

whenever
([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},r_{2}])\in\Gamma
, since
\Gamma\subset U_{q_{1}q_{2}}
, we have
r_{1}>0
.

Example 5.15
.

The function
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{GHK}}
in (
2.18
) satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
5.13
:
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{GHK}}
is a finite, radially
1
-homogeneous, convex and continuous function and condition (
5.16
) is satisfied with
\varrho_{i}=1
. Condition (1) is clearly satisfied. Condition (3) follows by

\frac{\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{GHK}}([x_{1},r_{1}],[x_{2},1])-\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{GHK}}(\mathfrak{o}_{1},[x_{2},1])}{r_{1}}=1-\frac{2}{\sqrt{r_{1}}}e^{-|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}/2}\to-\infty\text{ as }r_{1}\downarrow 0,

for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Condition (2) is easily seen to be satisfied since, if

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x_{1}}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{GHK}}([x_{1},1],[y,q])\\
\partial_{r_{1}}\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{GHK}}([x_{1},1],[y,q])\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{q}e^{-|x_{1}-y|^{2}/2}(x_{1}-y)\\
1-\sqrt{q}e^{-|x_{1}-y|^{2}/2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}v_{0}\\
c_{0}\end{pmatrix}

for some
(v_{0},c_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
, then

[y,q]=\left[x_{1}-v_{0}(1-c_{0})^{-1},(1-c_{0})^{2}e^{|v_{0}|^{2}(1-c_{0})^{-2}}\right]\text{ if }c_{0}\neq 1,\quad[y,q]=\mathfrak{o}_{2}\text{ if }c_{0}=1.

Appendix A
Metric and topological properties

In this section we study a few metric and topological properties of the Unbalanced Optimal Transport functional assuming that the cost function is related to a metric on the cone. As usual we fix a completely regular space
\mathsf{X}
and an exponent
p\in[1,+\infty)
. We consider a function
\varrho:\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X},\mathsf{X}]\to[0,+\infty]

such that

(A.1)

\begin{split}\varrho\text{ is an extended metric on }\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]\text{ such that }\varrho^{p}\text{ is radially $1$-homogeneous, proper and lsc.}\end{split}

We consider the Unbalanced Optimal Transport functional induced by
\varrho^{p}
on
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})
and the (extended) Wasserstein
p
-metric
[
Vil09
,
AGS08
]
induced by
\varrho
on
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}])
.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\varrho
be as in (
A.1
). We define
\mathcal{D}_{\varrho,p}:\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X})\to[0,+\infty]
and
W_{\varrho,p}:\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}])\times\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}])\to[0,+\infty]
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\varrho,p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\mathscr{U}_{\varrho^{p}}^{1/p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\left(\min\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X},\mathsf{X}]}\varrho^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\alpha}:\bm{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}^{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right\}\right)^{1/p},\quad\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}),

\displaystyle W_{\varrho,p}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}):=\mathsf{OT}_{\varrho^{p}}^{1/p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\left(\min\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X},\mathsf{X}]}\varrho^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}:\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\right\}\right)^{1/p},\quad\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]),

where
\mathsf{OT}_{\varrho^{p}}
is as in Definition
3.20
.
Finally we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\varrho,p}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}])

\displaystyle:=\left\{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]:\int_{\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}]}\varrho^{p}(\mathfrak{y},\mathfrak{o})\,\mathrm{d}\alpha(\mathfrak{y})<+\infty\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\varrho,p}(\mathsf{X})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathsf{X}):\int_{\mathsf{X}}\varrho^{p}([x,1],\mathfrak{o})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<+\infty\right\}.

Remark A.2
.

If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\varrho,p}(\mathsf{X})
then every
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}])
such that
\mathfrak{h}^{1}(\alpha)=\mu
is an element of
\mathcal{P}_{\varrho,p}(\mathfrak{C}[\mathsf{X}])
.

Remark A.3
.

As explained in Remark
2.13
, we limit our analysis to the case in which
\varrho^{p}
is radially
1
-homogeneous. In case
\varrho^{p}
is radially
q
-homogeneous,
q\in(1,+\infty)
,
